Convergence Rates for Learning Pseudo-Differential Operators
Jiaheng Chen and Daniel Sanz-Alonso

Abstract
This paper establishes convergence rates for learning elliptic pseudo-differential operators, a fundamental operator class in partial differential equations and mathematical physics. In a wavelet–Galerkin framework, we formulate learning over this class as a structured infinite-dimensional regression problem with multiscale sparsity. Building on this structure, we propose a sparse, data- and computation-efficient estimator, which leverages a novel matrix compression scheme tailored to the learning task and a nested-support strategy to balance approximation and estimation errors. In addition to obtaining convergence rates for the estimator, we show that the learned operator induces an efficient and stable Galerkin solver whose numerical error matches its statistical accuracy. Our results therefore contribute to bringing together operator learning, data-driven solvers, and wavelet methods in scientific computing.

algorithmsection

Keywords.
Operator learning; pseudo-differential operators; wavelets

MSC codes.
62G05; 65T60; 35S05

1
Introduction

Operator learning
is an emerging paradigm at the intersection of scientific computing, partial differential equations (PDEs), and machine learning
[
96
,
5
,
104
,
103
,
116
,
90
,
97
,
17
,
88
,
133
]
. In a supervised learning setting, the task can be formulated as follows: for an unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
, we observe noisy input–output pairs
\{(u_{i},f_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfying

(1.1)

\displaystyle f_{i}=\mathcal{A}u_{i}+w_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,

where
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
models the noise. The goal is to estimate
\mathcal{A}
accurately and efficiently from the data, under a prescribed error metric and modeling choices for the inputs, outputs, and noise.

This paper establishes convergence rates for learning elliptic pseudo-differential operators (PDOs), a broad and fundamental operator class in analysis and PDEs
[
74
,
125
,
136
,
145
]
. PDOs encompass both differential and integral operators and naturally capture nonlocality and ill-conditioning; canonical examples include Green’s operators, Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps, and boundary integral operators. We assume that the inputs
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and noise terms
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are Gaussian random functions (including Matérn classes) with prescribed Sobolev smoothness, and we measure error in an operator norm between Sobolev spaces.

Motivated by the (quasi-)sparse representation of PDOs in wavelet coordinates and by wavelet–Galerkin methods in scientific computing
[
11
,
51
,
122
,
39
,
45
,
33
,
34
,
68
]
, we propose a sparse, data- and computation-efficient wavelet-based estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and show that, with high probability and up to poly-logarithmic factors,

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\;\lesssim\;N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}.

The exponent
\rho\geq 0
depends on the Sobolev indices
(t,t^{\prime})
in the error metric
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
, the order of the operator
\mathcal{A}
, the spatial dimension, the smoothness of the inputs and noise, and wavelet regularity/approximation order parameters. Our theory also covers the noiseless setting (
w_{i}\equiv 0
in (
1.1
)), in which case the estimator achieves super-algebraic convergence. Finally, we show that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
yields an efficient and stable wavelet–Galerkin solver whose numerical error matches its statistical accuracy, thereby bridging operator learning, data-driven PDE solvers, and wavelet methods in scientific computing.

1.1
Main Contributions and Outline

•

Learning PDOs in a wavelet–Galerkin framework.

We formulate the study of learning PDOs in a wavelet–Galerkin framework, casting the operator learning task as the estimation of the operator’s bi-infinite wavelet matrix representation. By doing so, we reduce the continuous operator learning problem to a structured infinite-dimensional matrix regression problem.

•

Structured infinite-dimensional regression with multiscale sparsity.

We propose a sparse, data- and computation-efficient estimator for the wavelet–Galerkin discretization of the unknown operator by exploiting the (quasi-)sparse representation of PDOs in wavelet coordinates. Two key ingredients are:

(i)

Learning-oriented matrix compression.

We introduce a new compression scheme tailored to the operator-learning error metric, identifying a target sparse support that captures the essential wavelet coefficients up to the desired accuracy. Our goal-oriented compression scheme enables sharper learning guarantees than classical methods in scientific computing.

(ii)

Nested-support regression.

To mitigate data-induced
omitted-variable bias
when regressing on the target support, we fit each column on a carefully enlarged regression support and then restrict back to the target one. The enlargement is calibrated so that the omitted-variable bias is reduced to (at most) the level of the truncation and compression error, while the variance remains of the same order. This nested-support regression approach provides a principled way to balance data-induced bias against the approximation error from truncation and compression, and may be of independent interest for other structured learning problems.

•

Convergence rates for learning elliptic PDOs.
Under general assumptions on the inputs, noise, and the choice of wavelets, our first main result, Theorem
5.1
, establishes a nearly optimal high-probability error bound of order
N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}
(up to poly-logarithmic factors) for learning elliptic PDOs. The bound exhibits a
parametric regime
, achieving the rate
N^{-1/2}
(i.e.,
\rho=0
) when the error metric is sufficiently weak; otherwise
\rho>0
, yielding a strictly
nonparametric
rate. The estimator is optimally sparse in wavelet coordinates, containing only
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
nonzero entries, and can be computed in nearly
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})
time, where
2^{Jn}
is the dimension of the learned Galerkin matrix and
N
is the sample size; under the bias–variance optimal choice of the truncation level
J
(as a function of
N
), this corresponds to
\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\big)
nonzero coefficients and a total runtime of
\mathcal{O}\big(N^{1+\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\big)
, where
n
is the spatial dimension and
r
is the order of the PDO. The proof of Theorem
5.1
hinges on a sharp multiscale error analysis that controls truncation and compression errors, as well as omitted-variable bias and variance. A key technical contribution is to combine sharp probabilistic analysis with a refined characterization of the blockwise multiscale sparsity pattern induced by our new learning-oriented matrix compression scheme. Our theory also covers the noiseless setting, where our estimator attains a super-algebraic convergence rate. As a corollary of our general results, we obtain in Example
5.7
what is, to our knowledge, the first explicit and nearly optimal tradeoff between statistical accuracy and computational cost for learning Green’s functions of elliptic PDEs —in general dimensions and for general differential order— from generic noisy Gaussian data. We also sharpen existing accuracy–cost tradeoffs in the noiseless setting to a nearly optimal one.

•

Convergence rates for data-driven PDE solver.

The learned sparse operator induces an efficient and stable wavelet–Galerkin solver. Our second main result, Theorem
6.2
, shows that the solver’s numerical error inherits the statistical accuracy of the operator estimator (up to poly-logarithmic factors), and thus decays at an algebraic rate in the sample size
N
. Theorem
6.2
therefore provides a principled bridge between operator learning, data-driven PDE solvers, and wavelet–Galerkin methods in scientific computing.

The paper is organized as follows. After discussing related work and setting notation in the rest of the introduction, we formalize our operator learning problem in Section
2
. Then, in Section
3
, we formulate operator learning in a wavelet–Galerkin framework. Section
4
defines our estimator using learning-oriented matrix compression and nested-support regression. Convergence rates for learning elliptic PDOs and for data-driven PDE solvers are established in Sections
5
and
6
. Section
7
closes with conclusions and directions for future work.

1.2
Related Work

Operator learning

There is a vast and rapidly growing literature on operator learning, and a comprehensive overview is beyond the scope of this paper. Representative examples include neural-network-based approaches such as Fourier neural operators
[
96
,
116
,
90
]
, DeepONets
[
103
,
104
,
92
]
, and physics-informed neural operators
[
97
,
80
]
; kernel-based methods
[
128
,
10
,
77
,
102
,
147
,
150
,
79
]
; and random feature methods
[
111
,
112
,
98
,
149
]
. Related lines of work include learning Green’s functions
[
16
,
128
,
121
,
15
,
14
,
142
]
, operator learning for dynamical systems (e.g., Koopman operators and generators)
[
19
,
86
,
85
,
42
,
84
,
101
]
, structured covariance and precision operator estimation
[
3
,
4
,
2
,
29
]
, and applications to inverse problems
[
108
,
65
,
113
]
. From a theoretical perspective, there has been substantial recent interest in approximation-theoretic foundations
[
31
,
103
,
89
,
92
,
55
,
90
,
94
,
93
,
95
,
91
,
53
]
, as well as in statistical and data-complexity analyses of operator learning problems
[
109
,
54
,
78
,
87
,
100
,
132
,
118
,
1
]
. Below, we focus on the works most closely related to the present paper, and we refer the reader to recent surveys for broader perspectives
[
17
,
88
,
133
]
.

The paper
[
54
]
studies linear operator learning from noisy data in the setting where the operator is diagonalizable in a known basis, thereby reducing the problem to eigenvalue learning, and establishes posterior contraction rates in a Bayesian framework. The work
[
78
]
investigates the statistical limits of learning a Hilbert–Schmidt operator between two infinite-dimensional Sobolev reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces via ridge-regression-type estimators, deriving matching upper and lower bounds under the Hilbert–Schmidt norm; their analysis reduces to estimating an infinite-dimensional coefficient matrix with a polynomial-type entrywise decay profile. In contrast to
[
54
,
78
]
, we study the learning of elliptic PDOs under the
operator norm
between Sobolev spaces. Such a worst-case guarantee, rather than an error measured in a more “average” sense, is directly compatible with stability and error analyses for data-driven PDE solvers and aligns naturally with classical wavelet–Galerkin methods. From a technical standpoint, our problem is intrinsically non-diagonal: the bi-infinite wavelet matrix of a PDO exhibits a multiscale (quasi-)sparse structure, rather than an entrywise polynomial decay profile, giving rise to a highly structured infinite-dimensional regression problem.

A related line of work
[
16
,
14
]
considers the recovery of Green’s functions (solution operators) for elliptic PDEs via randomized numerical linear algebra, and obtains exponential convergence in the sample size by exploiting the low-rank structure on well-separated domains together with off-diagonal decay of the Green’s function. Their setting is noiseless (i.e.,
w_{i}\equiv 0
in (
1.1
)) and relies on carefully designed inputs aligned with the hierarchical low-rank structure of the Green’s function, whereas our framework covers a unified class of integral and differential operators and allows noisy observations with generic Gaussian-process input data. Moreover, their analysis is specialized to the three-dimensional case and their error bounds depend on the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the solution operator, which can be infinite in higher dimensions since Green’s functions may not be square-integrable. By contrast, our wavelet framework treats general dimensions in a unified way and works with general Sobolev-to-Sobolev operator norms. More recently,
[
118
]
establishes general convergence guarantees for least-squares empirical risk minimizers over general operator classes in terms of their approximation properties and metric entropy bounds, via empirical process techniques. By comparison, we focus on a physically meaningful, PDE-inspired operator class and derive convergence rates through an explicit wavelet-based multiscale regression analysis that leverages the compressibility of PDOs in wavelet coordinates; moreover, our estimator is sparse and computationally efficient.

We also mention neural-network architectures inspired by PDOs, or designed to represent/approximate PDOs
[
64
,
20
,
66
,
148
,
124
,
30
]
. Our contribution is complementary: to the best of our knowledge, we provide the first rigorous statistical convergence rates for learning PDOs. While our estimator can be viewed through the lens of functional linear regression
[
117
,
67
]
, the key novelty is that the coefficient structure is governed by
a priori
operator-analytic bounds on wavelet representations of PDOs, yielding a multiscale compressibility pattern that we exploit both statistically and computationally.

Wavelet–Garlekin methods

Wavelet methods in scientific computing and numerical analysis have a long history; see, e.g.,
[
105
,
11
,
7
,
52
,
44
,
49
,
48
,
71
,
51
,
33
,
41
,
70
]
. We refer the reader to the books and reviews
[
52
,
51
,
39
,
40
,
122
,
131
]
. More recently, related ideas have been deployed in other settings, such as multilevel approximation of Gaussian random fields
[
72
,
68
,
6
]
.
Three features of wavelets are particularly central in numerical analysis. First, wavelet systems characterize Sobolev (and Besov) spaces in the sense that, under suitable constructions, they form Riesz bases for a range of Sobolev spaces. This property yields simple
diagonal preconditioners
for differential and integral operators discretized in wavelet bases via Galerkin methods
[
47
,
76
,
51
]
. Second, vanishing moments (cancellation property) allow wavelets to exploit operator smoothness, leading to
sparse
or
compressible
representations for broad classes of operators
[
11
,
44
,
49
,
48
,
51
,
129
]
, in both standard and non-standard forms. Third, wavelets support adaptivity and nonlinear approximation, enabling optimal finite-term approximations and sparse representations of functions
[
56
,
57
,
33
,
41
]
. Together, these principles underpin fast, adaptive, multiscale numerical algorithms that leverage approximate sparsity. Beyond numerical analysis, wavelet ideas have had a lasting impact across applied mathematics and data science, including statistical estimation via thresholding
[
59
,
60
,
58
,
61
]
, data compression in image processing (e.g., JPEG 2000)
[
35
,
135
,
126
,
134
]
, compressed sensing
[
62
,
26
]
, and, more recently, connections to neural networks
[
18
,
106
]
.

While much of the classical wavelet literature is devoted to numerical simulation and analysis, the present work studies operator learning through a wavelet lens, with the goal of establishing statistical convergence rates for learning an unknown operator from noisy input–output data pairs. Our estimator targets the bi-infinite wavelet matrix representation of the operator and crucially leverages diagonal preconditioning and the (quasi-)sparsity of PDOs in wavelet coordinates, while exhibiting several features that do not arise in classical wavelet–Galerkin PDE solvers. We emphasize the following distinctions from the classical wavelet–Galerkin methods. First, in contrast to the matrix compression schemes used in
[
122
,
45
,
68
]
, we introduce a learning-oriented compression scheme that identifies the matrix entries to be estimated in a way tailored to the statistical learning task. Second, we develop a nested-support regression strategy to construct the estimator and mitigate the omitted-variable bias induced by the data —an effect absent in the context of deterministic numerical discretizations. In addition, we incorporate a further symmetrization step that exploits the symmetry of the unknown operator to reduce variance. Third, our error analysis requires a refined characterization of the blockwise sparsity structure of the wavelet matrix: beyond the number of nonzero entries (nnz) at a global level, we quantify sparsity within each block of wavelet coefficients corresponding to a given pair of scales. This refinement is essential in operator learning because, under the operator norm, the variance of our estimator aggregates across scales in a way that is not captured by global nnz alone. Finally, in Section
6
we discuss data-driven PDE solvers that use the learned sparse operator as a surrogate, further clarifying the connections to —and distinctions from— classical wavelet–Galerkin methods. Overall, this work contributes to bridging operator learning, data-driven PDE solvers, and wavelet–Galerkin methods.

1.3
Notation

Throughout this paper,
\mathcal{M}
will denote a smooth, closed, and connected orientable Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
immersed into Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
for some
D>n.

The support of a real-valued function
\phi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
\mathrm{supp}(\phi):=\overline{\{x\in\mathcal{M}:\phi(x)\neq 0\}}
, where the closure is taken in
\mathcal{M}
. If for some subset
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}
, we say that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is compactly included in
\mathcal{M}
and write
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\Subset\mathcal{M}
. The space of all smooth real-valued functions on
\mathcal{M}
is denoted by
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, and
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\subset C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
denotes the subspace of smooth functions
\phi
whose support satisfies
\mathrm{supp}(\phi)\Subset\mathcal{M}
. The space
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
consists of all square-integrable functions with respect to the intrinsic measure on
\mathcal{M}
, and its inner product is denoted by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})}
. The Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\mathcal{M}
is denoted by
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
. For any
s\in[0,\infty)
, we denote by
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
the Sobolev space of order
s
on
\mathcal{M}
, defined via the spectral decomposition of the Laplace–Beltrami operator; its norm is written
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})}
. For
s>0
, we denote by
H^{-s}(\mathcal{M})
the dual space of
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
, and write
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the duality pairing between
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
and
H^{-s}(\mathcal{M})
. For notational simplicity, we often omit the dependence on
\mathcal{M}
when referring to Sobolev spaces,
and the dependence on
s
when referring to duality pairings, whenever no ambiguity can arise. On occasion, we will work on an open bounded domain rather than a manifold, in which case we will make explicit the dependence of the function spaces on the domain.

For a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we denote by
\|x\|_{2}
its Euclidean norm and by
\|x\|_{\max}
its entry-wise maximum norm. For a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}},
we write
\|B\|
for its spectral (operator) norm,
\|B\|_{1}
its matrix
\ell_{1}
norm (maximum absolute column sum),
\|B\|_{\infty}
its matrix
\ell_{\infty}
norm (maximum absolute row sum), and
\|B\|_{\max}
for its entry-wise maximum norm. More generally, for a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}}
indexed by sets
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
(possibly infinite), we interpret
B
as a linear operator
\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{2})\to\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{1})
and let
\|B\|
denote its operator norm. For a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix, we denote by
\sigma_{\min}(B)
and
\sigma_{\max}(B)
its smallest and largest eigenvalues, respectively. For a finite set
\Lambda
, we write
|\Lambda|
for its cardinality.

Given two positive sequences
\{a_{k}\}
and
\{b_{k}\}
, we write
a_{k}\lesssim b_{k}
if there exists a constant
c>0
, independent of
k
, such that
a_{k}\leq c\,b_{k}
for all
k
. If both
a_{k}\lesssim b_{k}
and
b_{k}\lesssim a_{k}
hold, we write
a_{k}\asymp b_{k}
. If the constant
c
depends on some parameter
\tau
, we write
a_{k}\lesssim_{\tau}b_{k},b_{k}\lesssim_{\tau}a_{k}
, and
a_{k}\asymp_{\tau}b_{k}
to indicate this dependence.

2
Problem Setup

This section provides background on PDOs and formalizes our operator learning problem.

2.1
Pseudo-Differential Operators

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open bounded domain. For
r\in\mathbb{R},
the class
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{D})
of
pseudo-differential operators
(PDOs) of order
r
consists of all linear maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}),\quad(\mathcal{A}u)(x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}a(x,\xi)u^{\rm{ft}}(\xi)d\xi,

where
u^{\rm{ft}}(\xi):=\int_{\mathcal{D}}u(x)e^{-ix\cdot\xi}dx
denotes the Fourier transform of
u
, and
a(x,\xi)
is a
symbol
belonging to the Hörmander class
\mathsf{S}^{r}(\mathcal{D})
. The symbol class
\mathsf{S}^{r}(\mathcal{D})
consists of all functions
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that, for all
K\Subset\mathcal{D}
and every pair of multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, there exists a constant
C_{K,\alpha,\beta}>0
with

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\partial^{\beta}_{x}a(x,\xi)|\leq C_{K,\alpha,\beta}(1+|\xi|)^{r-|\alpha|},\quad\text{ for all }x\in K,\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Here
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
denotes
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers, and

|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}.

Intuitively, the order
r\in\mathbb{R}
characterizes the regularity properties of operators in the class
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{D}):
positive order corresponds to differential operators, while negative order corresponds to integral (smoothing) operators.

For a manifold
\mathcal{M}
, the class
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
is defined in the usual way by lifting to
\mathcal{M}
via local coordinates
[
136
]
. Specifically, a linear operator
\mathcal{B}:C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
is said to belong to
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
if, for any finite smooth partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{i}):i=1,\ldots,M\}
with respect to an atlas
\{\mathcal{M}_{i},\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
of
\mathcal{M}
, all transported operators

f\mapsto B_{i,i^{\prime}}f:=[(\mathcal{B}[\chi_{i}(f\circ\gamma_{i}^{-1})])\chi_{i^{\prime}}]\circ\gamma_{i^{\prime}}

belong to
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}\big(\gamma_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{i})\big)
for all
i,i^{\prime}=1,\ldots,M
.
\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
is thus invariantly defined and does not depend on the choice of atlas
[
74
,
136
]
. We refer the reader to
[
68
, Section A.2.2]
for more details. In this paper, we assume throughout that
\mathcal{M}
is an
n
-dimensional smooth closed manifold, e.g.
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}^{n}
(the
n
-torus) and
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{S}^{n}
(the
n
-sphere); extensions of our results to domains with boundary can be obtained by imposing appropriate boundary conditions.

For
t,t^{\prime}>r/2
, we will consider the
operator norm
of
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}
given by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}:=\sup_{\|w\|_{H^{t}}\leq 1}\|\mathcal{A}w\|_{H^{-t^{\prime}}}=\sup_{\|w\|_{H^{t}}\leq 1,\|v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\leq 1}|\langle\mathcal{A}w,v\rangle|,

where the duality pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is taken between
H^{t^{\prime}}
and
H^{-t^{\prime}}
.

2.2
Operator Learning

Consider the statistical model

(2.2)

\displaystyle f_{i}=\mathcal{A}u_{i}+w_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,

where
\mathcal{A}
is an unknown operator,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(u_{i},f_{i})}}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
are given data pairs, and
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are noise terms.
We are interested in the
operator learning problem
:

Given data pairs
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(u_{i},f_{i})}}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
, estimate the unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
under the norm (
2.1
).

We will work under the following standing assumption on the unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
, the input functions
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, and the noise terms
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.

Assumption 2.1

(Operator, input function, and noise)
.

(i)

Operator assumption:
For some given
r\in\mathbb{R},

\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M}).
Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is self-adjoint and positive definite in the sense that

\langle\mathcal{A}v,v\rangle\gtrsim\|v\|^{2}_{H^{r/2}},\quad\forall\ v\in H^{r/2}.

(ii)

Input function assumption:
The input functions
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are i.i.d. samples from a centered Gaussian process on
\mathcal{M}
with covariance operator
\mathcal{C}_{u}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{-2r_{1}}(\mathcal{M})
for some given
r_{1}>n/2+\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,r}}\right\}
. The operator
\mathcal{C}_{u}
is self-adjoint and positive definite, satisfying

\langle\mathcal{C}_{u}v,v\rangle\gtrsim\|v\|^{2}_{H^{-r_{1}}},\quad\forall\ v\in H^{-r_{1}}.

(iii)

Noise assumption:
The noise terms
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are i.i.d. samples from a centered Gaussian process on
\mathcal{M}
, independent of
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, with covariance operator
\mathcal{C}_{w}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{-2r_{2}}(\mathcal{M})
for some given
r_{2}>n/2
.
The operator
\mathcal{C}_{w}
is self-adjoint and positive definite, satisfying

\langle\mathcal{C}_{w}v,v\rangle\gtrsim\|v\|^{2}_{H^{-r_{2}}},\quad\forall\ v\in H^{-r_{2}}.

Our theory also covers the noiseless data setting, where
f_{i}=\mathcal{A}u_{i},1\leq i\leq N;
see Remark
5.6
. Notice that we assume the parameters
r,r_{1},r_{2}
that determine the order of
\mathcal{A}
and of the input/noise covariance operators to be given. In practice, these parameters may need to be estimated from data (see, e.g.,
[
68
, Section 5.4]
and
[
83
]
). The study of adaptive estimators agnostic to these nuisance parameters is an interesting direction for future work.

Remark 2.2
(Interpretation of Assumption
2.1
and examples).

Operator
\mathcal{A}
.
Under Assumption
2.1
(i), the operator
\mathcal{A}
is a continuous bijection from
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
to
H^{s-r}(\mathcal{M})
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
; see
[
68
, Proposition 11]
. Moreover, for any real exponent
\varrho
, the fractional power satisfies
\mathcal{A}^{\varrho}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{\varrho r}(\mathcal{M})
; see
[
68
, Proposition 12]
.

A canonical class of examples is given by Schrödinger (Hamiltonian) elliptic operators and their fractional powers:

\mathcal{H}:=-\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}+V(x),\qquad\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{H}^{\varrho},

where the potential
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
is uniformly positive, i.e.
V(x)\geq v_{\min}>0
. This includes both differential operators (
\varrho>0
) and integral/smoothing operators (
\varrho<0
).

Assumption
2.1
(i) is also compatible with a broad class of self-adjoint elliptic boundary integral operators arising from PDEs. In particular, when

\mathcal{M}=\partial\mathcal{D}
is a closed boundary surface of an
(n+1)
-dimensional domain
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, this class includes boundary integral equations of the first kind for the Laplacian as well as boundary integral formulations associated with the
Navier–Lamé and Stokes systems; see

[
45
,
70
,
51
]
for further background.
We believe that the results of this paper extend to broader classes of pseudo-differential and boundary integral operators, including operators that are not self-adjoint and/or not strongly elliptic. We leave these extensions to future work.

Another related (but slightly different) example is provided by infinitesimal generators of reversible diffusions. For the overdamped Langevin diffusion
dX_{t}=-\nabla U(X_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2/\eta}\,dW_{t}
, the generator
\mathcal{G}=-\nabla U\cdot\nabla+\eta^{-1}\Delta
is a second-order elliptic differential operator (hence a PDO), but it is generally not self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
. Instead, it is symmetric in the weighted space

L^{2}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M})
, where
\pi(x)\propto e^{-\eta U(x)}
is the stationary measure. Indeed,

\langle f,(-\mathcal{G})g\rangle_{\pi}=\eta^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\nabla f\cdot\nabla g\,\pi\,dx=\langle(-\mathcal{G})f,g\rangle_{\pi}.

Hence
-\mathcal{G}
is self-adjoint and positive semi-definite on
L^{2}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M})
(with a nullspace consisting of constants). We leave a detailed treatment of learning this class of operators using the techniques developed in this paper to future work.

Input function
u
and noise
w
.
Assumption
2.1
(ii)–(iii) states that the input functions
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and the noise terms
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are centered Gaussian processes, sampled independently as
u_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{u}),w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{w}).
The assumptions on
\mathcal{C}_{u}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w}
imply that
u_{i}
and
w_{i}
admit variational representations as solutions of coloring-operator equations driven by spatial white noise, with coloring operators in the Hörmander classes:

\mathcal{L}_{u}u=\mathcal{W}_{u},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{w}w=\mathcal{W}_{w},

where
\mathcal{L}_{u}=\mathcal{C}_{u}^{-1/2}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r_{1}}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{L}_{w}=\mathcal{C}_{w}^{-1/2}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r_{2}}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\mathcal{W}_{u},\mathcal{W}_{w}
are independent Gaussian white noises on
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
; see
[
68
, (2.1), Proposition 1]
. This setting includes Whittle–Matérn-type Gaussian random functions widely used in modeling and applications
[
143
,
107
,
144
,
99
,
127
]
. For instance, one may take

\mathcal{L}_{u}:=(\tau_{u}(x)I-\nabla\cdot(\kappa_{u}(x)\nabla))^{r_{1}/2},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{w}:=(\tau_{w}(x)I-\nabla\cdot(\kappa_{w}(x)\nabla))^{r_{2}/2},

where
\tau_{u},\tau_{w}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
are uniformly positive, i.e.
\tau_{u}(x)\geq\tau_{u,\min}>0
and
\tau_{w}(x)\geq\tau_{w,\min}>0
, and
\kappa_{u},\kappa_{w}
are smooth and uniformly elliptic (e.g. smooth symmetric positive-definite matrix fields); see
[
120
]
and
[
68
, Appendix C]
.

The parameters
r_{1},r_{2}
control Sobolev regularity. Spectral asymptotics of
\mathcal{C}_{u}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w}
(via Weyl’s law), see
[
43
,
72
,
68
]
, imply that

u_{i}\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{ for all }s<r_{1}-n/2\quad\text{a.s.},\qquad w_{i}\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{ for all }s<r_{2}-n/2\quad\text{a.s.}

Thus, the conditions
r_{1}>n/2
and
r_{2}>n/2
ensure
u_{i},w_{i}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
almost surely. Moreover, since
\mathcal{A}:H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\to H^{s-r}(\mathcal{M})
is an isomorphism for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathcal{A}u_{i}\in H^{s-r}(\mathcal{M})\quad\text{ for all }s<r_{1}-n/2\quad\text{a.s.},

so
\mathcal{A}u_{i}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
whenever
r_{1}-n/2-r>0
. Combining these observations, under the assumptions

r_{1}>n/2+\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,r}}\right\},\qquad r_{2}>n/2,

all functions in the statistical model (
2.2
) —namely
u_{i},w_{i}
, and
f_{i}=\mathcal{A}u_{i}+w_{i}
— belong to
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
almost surely for every
1\leq i\leq N
.
∎

3
Wavelet–Matrix Formulation of Operator Learning

In this section, we recast the operator learning problem in terms of learning a bi-infinite wavelet matrix representation. To that end, we discretize the continuous operator-function model (
2.2
) into a discrete, infinite-dimensional matrix-vector model, using a biorthogonal wavelet system.

3.1
Biorthogonal Wavelets

Here, we briefly summarize the biorthogonal wavelet framework used in this paper; additional details, properties, and a sketch of the construction of the associated multiresolution analyses (MRAs) can be found in Appendix
A.1
. Let

\Psi=\{\psi_{j,k}:j\geq j_{0},\,k\in\nabla_{j}\},\qquad\widetilde{\Psi}=\{\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}:j\geq j_{0},\,k\in\nabla_{j}\}

denote a pair of biorthogonal wavelet bases of
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
with
|\nabla_{j}|\asymp 2^{jn}.
The indices
\lambda=(j,k)
encode the information about scale
(j)
and location
(k)
.
We introduce the wavelet index set

\mathcal{J}:=\{\lambda=(j,k):j\geq j_{0},\,k\in\nabla_{j}\}.

Any function
u\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
admits expansions in the primal and dual bases given by

\displaystyle u=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\Psi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}u_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda},\qquad u=\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Psi}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{u}_{\lambda}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda},

where
u_{\lambda}:=\langle u,\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\rangle
and
\widetilde{u}_{\lambda}:=\langle u,\psi_{\lambda}\rangle.

The
bi-infinite wavelet matrix representation

\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}}
of
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
is defined by

\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}:=\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle,\quad\forall\ \lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}.

The action of
\mathcal{A}
on
u
can be expressed in the dual basis by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}u=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u})_{\lambda}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}.

Using biorthogonality, the pairing of
\mathcal{A}u
with
v=\mathbf{v}^{\top}\Psi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}v_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}
is given by

\langle\mathcal{A}u,v\rangle=\bigg\langle\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u})_{\lambda}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda},\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}v_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\bigg\rangle=\langle\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}.

Consequently,
\mathcal{A}
admits the (formal) wavelet expansion

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}}\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

The operator norm of
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}
defined in (
2.1
) is equivalent to a discrete norm for its bi-infinite matrix representation
\mathbf{A}
. This equivalence between continuous and discrete norms, and the related idea of preconditioning, will play a key role in our theory.
Specifically, for each index
\lambda=(j,k)
, let
|\lambda|:=j
and define the bi-infinite diagonal scaling matrix

\mathbf{D}^{s}:=\mathrm{diag}\Big(2^{s|\lambda|}:\lambda\in\mathcal{J}\Big),\qquad s\in\mathbb{R}.

For
w=\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Psi\in H^{t}(\mathcal{M})
and
v=\mathbf{v}^{\top}\Psi\in H^{t^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M})
, the wavelet characterization of Sobolev norms (Lemma
A.1
(iv)) yields

\|w\|^{2}_{H^{t}}\asymp\|\mathbf{D}^{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\|v\|^{2}_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\asymp\|\mathbf{D}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\text{if }\,t,t^{\prime}\in(-\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma),

where
\gamma
and
\widetilde{\gamma}
are primal/dual regularity parameters. Hence, the norm (
2.1
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|w\|_{H^{t}}\leq 1,\|v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\leq 1}|\langle\mathcal{A}w,v\rangle|

\displaystyle\asymp\sup_{\|\mathbf{D}^{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\leq 1,\|\mathbf{D}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\leq 1}|\langle\mathbf{A}\mathbf{w},\mathbf{v}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\mathbf{D}^{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\leq 1,\|\mathbf{D}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\leq 1}|\langle\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-t}\mathbf{D}^{t}\mathbf{w},\mathbf{D}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}\rangle|

(3.2)

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|,

where
\|\cdot\|
in the last line denotes the operator norm from
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
to
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
. Notice that since
\mathcal{A}
is self-adjoint under our standing Assumption
2.1
,
\mathbf{A}
is symmetric. Hence, we may assume that
t\leq t^{\prime}
in what follows without loss of generality; see Remark
5.2
.

As discussed in
[
51
]
and overviewed in Appendix
A.1
, a biorthogonal system
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
can be
characterized by four parameters

\gamma,\ \widetilde{\gamma}\quad\text{(primal/dual regularity)},\qquad d,\ \widetilde{d}\quad\text{(primal/dual approximation order)}.

The parameters
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}
control the Sobolev
regularity of the primal and dual wavelets, while
d,\widetilde{d}
correspond to the
number of vanishing moments and determine approximation accuracy.
For convenience, we now collect all the conditions on wavelet parameters that we will require. In addition to imposing constraints on the wavelet parameters, the following assumption specifies the admissible range for a parameter
\sigma
used in our estimation procedure and error bounds. This parameter is related to the off-diagonal decay of the wavelet coefficients of
\mathcal{A}
across scale separation, as will become clear in Proposition
A.2

(I)
(ii).

Assumption 3.1

(Wavelets)
.

We are given a pair
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
of biorthogonal wavelet systems with regularities
(\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma})
and approximation orders
(d,\widetilde{d})
satisfying:

(i)

\frac{r}{2},\,r_{1},\,-r_{2},\,t,\,t^{\prime}\in(-\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma).

(ii)

d>\max\{t,t^{\prime}\}
,
\widetilde{d}>-n/2-r/2
.

(iii)

\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\gamma-\frac{r}{2},\widetilde{\gamma}+\frac{r}{2},\widetilde{d}+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{r}{2}}}\right\}>\sigma>\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{n}{2}+\max\{t,t^{\prime}\}-\frac{r}{2},\frac{3n}{2}-t+\frac{r}{2},\ \frac{t^{\prime}+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r}{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}n}}\right\}
.

Throughout, we treat the parameters
n,r,r_{1},r_{2},t,t^{\prime}
as fixed.
Conditions (i)–(iii) in Assumption
3.1
can be satisfied by choosing biorthogonal wavelets with sufficient smoothness and sufficiently many vanishing moments,
i.e., by taking
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma},d,\widetilde{d}
sufficiently large relative to these fixed parameters.

3.2
Operator Learning in the Bi-Infinite Matrix Framework

Recall the statistical model (
2.2
).
In the dual basis we can write, for
1\leq i\leq N,

(3.3)

\displaystyle f_{i}=\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i}^{\top}\widetilde{\Psi}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}\langle f_{i},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda},\quad\mathcal{A}u_{i}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{\lambda}\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda},\quad w_{i}=\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{\top}\widetilde{\Psi}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}}\langle w_{i},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}.

Hence, (
2.2
) admits the following infinite-dimensional matrix–vector representation:

\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,

where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}}
is the bi-infinite matrix representation of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
are the wavelet coefficients of the output and noise functions
f_{i}
and
w_{i}
, respectively. In compact form,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{W},

where we used that
\mathbf{A}
is symmetric since
\mathcal{A}
is self-adjoint, and we defined

\mathbf{F}:=[\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{N}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\mathcal{J}},\quad\mathbf{U}:=[\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{N}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\mathcal{J}},\quad\mathbf{W}:=[\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{N}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\mathcal{J}}.

Thus, we have derived the following
bi-infinite matrix learning problem
:

Given
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{F}
, estimate the unknown bi-infinite matrix
\mathbf{A}

under the norm (
3.1
).

This bi-infinite matrix learning problem will play a crucial role in our operator learning theory: we will construct an estimator of
\mathcal{A}
from an estimator of its bi-infinite matrix
\mathbf{A},
and we will leverage the equivalence of the norms (
2.1
) and (
3.1
) in our analysis.
We postpone the construction of the estimator to Section
4
and its error analysis to Section
5
. Both the construction and the analysis leverage the following properties of the bi-infinite matrix
\mathbf{A}
, the data matrix
\mathbf{U}
, and the noise matrix
\mathbf{W}
ensuing from Assumption
2.1
and the wavelet discretization induced by
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
. These properties are standard
[
51
,
45
,
122
,
68
]
, but for completeness we include a brief formal derivation in Appendix
A.2
.

Proposition 3.2

(Properties of
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{U},\mathbf{W}
)
.

Under Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
3.1
(i)-(ii), the matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{U}
, and
\mathbf{W}
satisfy:

(I)

Approximate sparsity of
\mathbf{A}
:
For
(j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
, we define
S_{j,k}:=\mathrm{conv\,hull}\bigl(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k})\bigr)\subset\mathcal{M}
the convex hull of the support of
\psi_{j,k}
.

(i)

For all
\lambda=(j,k),\lambda^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{J}
such that
\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\gtrsim 2^{-\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}}
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}|\lesssim 2^{-(j+j^{\prime})(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{-(n+r+2\widetilde{d})}.

(ii)

For all
\lambda=(j,k),\lambda^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{J}
such that
\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\lesssim 2^{-\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}}
, and for any
0<\sigma<\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\gamma-r/2,\widetilde{\gamma}+r/2}}\right\}
,

|\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}|\lesssim 2^{(j+j^{\prime})r/2}2^{-\sigma|j-j^{\prime}|}.

(II)

Diagonal preconditioning:

There exist constants
0<c_{-}<c_{+}<\infty
such that:

(i)

\mathbf{A}
is a symmetric and positive definite operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
, and

c_{-}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-r/2})\leq\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{D}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-r/2})\leq c_{+}.

(ii)

The population covariance matrix of the input data,
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{u}:=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}/N}}\right]
, is a symmetric, positive definite, and compact operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
. Furthermore,

c_{-}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}^{r_{1}}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{u}\mathbf{D}^{r_{1}})\leq\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{D}^{r_{1}}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{u}\mathbf{D}^{r_{1}})\leq c_{+}.

(iii)

The population covariance matrix of the noise,
\mathbf{C}_{w}:=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}/N}}\right]
, is a symmetric, positive definite, and compact operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
. Furthermore,

c_{-}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}^{r_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{w}\mathbf{D}^{r_{2}})\leq\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{D}^{r_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{w}\mathbf{D}^{r_{2}})\leq c_{+}.

Remark 3.3
.

Combining Proposition
3.2

(I)
(i)–(ii) with Assumption
3.1
(ii), we obtain the following uniform bound for all
\lambda=(j,k),\lambda^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{J}
:

|\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}|\lesssim 2^{(j+j^{\prime})r/2}\cdot 2^{-\sigma|j-j^{\prime}|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2^{\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}}\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})}}\right)^{-(n+r+2\widetilde{d})}.

Such estimates are classical in the wavelet–Galerkin literature and underpin the construction of efficient numerical schemes; see, for example,
[
51
, (7.11), (9.27)]
and
[
33
, (2.28), (2.29)]
.
∎

4
Construction of the Estimator

In this section, we construct our estimator for the unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
in two stages. In the first stage, we use the
a priori
estimates from Proposition
3.2
to identify a subset of significant entries of the bi-infinite matrix representation of
\mathcal{A}.
In the second stage, we estimate these significant entries using the data. The final estimator of
\mathcal{A}
will take the form

(4.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

We will describe the two stages in turn. First, in Subsection
4.1
we introduce the truncation and compression procedure underlying the definition of the set
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
of significant entries to be estimated. Next, in Subsection
4.2
we define the entrywise estimates
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}
for
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
using a nested-support regression strategy.

4.1
First Stage: Truncation and Compression

This subsection introduces the set
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
of significant entries to be estimated. The key idea is that
any
bi-infinite matrix
\mathbf{A}
satisfying Proposition
3.2
can be well approximated by truncation into a finite-dimensional matrix and subsequent matrix compression. The set
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
corresponds to the non-zero entries of the compressed matrix, and is independent of
\mathbf{A}.
We emphasize that the truncation and compression procedures do not make use of the given data, and are based on
a priori
estimates.

Matrix truncation crops the operator discarding fine scales, as formalized in the following:

Definition 4.1

(Matrix truncation)
.

For
J>j_{0},
consider the set of wavelet indices with level at most
J,
given by

\Lambda_{J}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(j,k):j_{0}\leq j\leq J,k\in\nabla_{j}}}\right\}.

Given a bi-infinite matrix
\mathbf{A},
we define its
a priori
truncation

\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}:=(\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}.

In our later developments, truncation causes a bias in estimation of
\mathcal{A}
since we will only estimate (a subset of) entries of
\mathbf{A}
up to scale level
J.
The choice of
J
in terms of the sample size
N
will be determined through a bias–variance tradeoff. We will characterize the error induced by matrix truncation in Proposition
5.9
below.

Compression exploits the sparsity of the bi-infinite matrix representation
\mathbf{A}
of the PDO
\mathcal{A}
in the wavelet basis. In particular, Proposition
3.2

(I)
shows that matrix coefficients
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}=\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle
decay rapidly as the supports of the wavelets
\psi_{\lambda},\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}}
become separated or as their scales differ, motivating the following definition. Recall that for
(j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
,
S_{j,k}=\mathrm{conv\,hull}(\mathrm{supp}\bigl(\psi_{j,k})\bigr)
denotes the convex hull of the support of
\psi_{j,k}
.

Definition 4.2

(Matrix compression)
.

Given
J,t,t^{\prime},r,n,\sigma
and a biorthogonal wavelet pair
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
with regularity
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}
and approximation orders
d,\widetilde{d}
, define the support set

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})=\mathrm{supp}\big(J,t,t^{\prime},r,n,\sigma,\widetilde{d}\,\big)

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}:\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau_{jj^{\prime}},j\leq\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2}}J+\frac{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}-(t-\frac{r}{2})}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2}}j^{\prime},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\qquad j^{\prime}\leq\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t-\frac{r}{2}}J+\frac{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}-(t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2})}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t-\frac{r}{2}}j\bigg\},

where the threshold
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
is chosen as

(4.3)

\tau_{jj^{\prime}}:=a\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{2^{-\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}},2^{(J(t+t^{\prime}-r)-jt^{\prime}-j^{\prime}t-(j+j^{\prime})\widetilde{d})/(2\widetilde{d}+r)}}}\right\}

for some sufficiently large constant
a>1
.

The indicator matrix associated to
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
is

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}:=\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})}\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}},

where the entry
(\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}=1
if and only if
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
.

The
a priori
compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
is defined by

\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}:=\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}\odot\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}},

where
\odot
denotes the Hadamard (entrywise) product.

Figure 1:
Illustration of the
(J,t,t^{\prime})
-compression of Definition
4.2
in the
(j,j^{\prime})
–plane.
The index set is partitioned into regions
D_{1}
–
D_{6}
: blocks in
D_{1}\cup D_{2}
are discarded;
in
D_{3}
and
D_{4}
, entries are retained only when
\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
with

\tau_{jj^{\prime}}\asymp 2^{-j^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}\asymp 2^{-j}
, respectively; in
D_{5}
, entries are retained only when
\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
with

\tau_{jj^{\prime}}\asymp 2^{(J(t+t^{\prime}-r)-jt^{\prime}-j^{\prime}t-(j+j^{\prime})\widetilde{d})/(2\widetilde{d}+r)}
;
D_{6}
is uncompressed (all entries retained).

Throughout, we write
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
to emphasize its dependence on
(J,t,t^{\prime})
, suppressing the parameters
(r,n,\sigma,\widetilde{d})
that remain fixed.
In our later developments, matrix compression introduces a second layer of bias, which will be bounded in Proposition
5.10
.

Remark 4.3
(Discussion: matrix compression and comparison with
[
45
]
).

In wavelet methods for scientific computing and numerical analysis —particularly in boundary integral equation solvers— matrix compression techniques play a central role in achieving (near-)optimal computational complexity, and are a key ingredient in wavelet–Galerkin methods
[
51
,
39
,
34
,
45
,
70
,
68
]
. In the present work, motivated by operator learning rather than classical PDE solvers, we introduce a compression scheme specifically tailored to the statistical estimation setting. Below we discuss the motivation and key properties of the matrix compression in Definition
4.2
and compare it with the compression scheme developed in
[
45
]
; see
[
45
, Section 7]
.

1.

Key properties of matrix compression.

Proposition
5.10
shows that the weighted compression error is of order

2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
(up to a factor
J
), matching the truncation error
2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
induced by restricting to indices up to level
J
; see Definition
4.1
and Proposition
5.9
. This is a standard feature of wavelet compression schemes; see
[
45
, Theorem 9.1]
and
[
68
, Proposition 6]
.
Moreover, the total number of nonzero entries of the compressed matrix

\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
is of order
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
, i.e.,
linear in the number of wavelet indices
|\Lambda_{J}|\asymp 2^{Jn}
; see the bound

\mathrm{nnz}\big(\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}\big)\lesssim 2^{Jn}
in Proposition
C.2
.
This “linear complexity” property guarantees optimally sparse approximations and optimal computational complexity; see
[
45
, Theorem 11.1]
. It is used in
[
70
]
to obtain quadrature strategies with linear scaling, and analogous ideas appear in covariance/precision approximation in
[
68
]
.

2.

Choice of
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
.

The thresholding based on
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
exploits Proposition
3.2

(I)
(i), which implies that the matrix coefficients

\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}=\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle
decay rapidly as the supports of
\psi_{\lambda}
and
\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}}
separate. Accordingly, we keep only those entries with

\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
.

A key conceptual difference from the compression strategies in

[
45
,
68
]

(e.g.,
[
45
, Section 7]
and
[
68
, Definition 1]
)
is that in our learning setting
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
depends on the regularities
t,t^{\prime}
appearing in the error metric
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
, whereas in
[
45
,
68
]
the threshold depends instead on a user-specified hyperparameter
d^{\prime}\in(d,\widetilde{d}+r)
. For prescribed parameters
t
and
t^{\prime}
, our choice typically yields smaller values of
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
, leading to more aggressive entry deletion while preserving the same bias level
2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
. More precisely, since

d^{\prime}>d>\max\{t,t^{\prime}\}
,

2^{(J(t+t^{\prime}-r)-jt^{\prime}-j^{\prime}t-(j+j^{\prime})\widetilde{d})/(2\widetilde{d}+r)}\leq 2^{(2J(d^{\prime}-r/2)-(j+j^{\prime})(d^{\prime}+\widetilde{d}))/(2\widetilde{d}+r)},

and when
j,j^{\prime}<J
the inequality is strict; cf.
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
in
[
68
, (3.20)]
.

From a statistical perspective, since our estimator (defined later) targets the sparse compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
and is supported on
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
, its variance analysis depends crucially on the sparsity pattern induced by the compression scheme. Our learning-oriented compression is advantageous: within each block, it retains fewer coefficients (equivalently, a smaller support), which leads to a smaller variance bound. We emphasize that this refinement is essential for variance analysis in Proposition
5.12
, because under the matrix operator norm the variance aggregates across scales in a way that is not captured by the global number of nonzero entries alone. In particular, both our compression and the matrix compression scheme in
[
45
]
guarantee a global nnz of optimal order
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
, i.e., linear in
|\Lambda_{J}|
. However, obtaining a sharp variance bound requires near-optimal sparsity
within each block
; this is precisely where our learning-oriented compression provides an advantage.

3.

Slope conditions.

Beyond entrywise thresholding, we retain only those
(j,j^{\prime})
blocks satisfying the two slope conditions in (
4.2
), which exploit Proposition
3.2

(I)
(ii). By Assumption
3.1
(iii), we have
\sigma-n/2>\max\{t,t^{\prime}\}-r/2
and
\widetilde{d}>\sigma-n/2-r/2
, which ensures that the slope constraints define a nontrivial admissible region in the
(j,j^{\prime})
plane; see Figure
1
. As will become clear in the proof of Proposition
5.10
, these slope conditions are chosen sharply so that the discarded blocks in
D_{1}\cup D_{2}
contribute at most
2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
to the bias. In this sense, the slope conditions are deliberately aggressive: they discard as many blocks as possible while still ensuring that the resulting bias remains of order at most
2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
. At the same time, discarding more blocks reduces the number of retained coefficients and hence helps control the variance of the estimator.

In summary, both the choice of the thresholding levels
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
and the design of the slope conditions are guided by the same principle: keep as few entries as possible within each
(j,j^{\prime})
block while ensuring that the induced bias is at most of order
2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
under the weighted matrix operator norm
\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\cdot)\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|
in (
3.1
), matching the truncation bias introduced in Definition
4.1
. This is why
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
and the slope conditions depend on the prescribed
(t,t^{\prime})
in the learning error metric. By contrast, the matrix compression scheme in
[
45
]
is designed to accommodate a range of weight parameters simultaneously (up to the approximation order of the MRAs); see
[
45
, Theorem 9.1]
and
[
68
, Proposition 6]
.

4.

Asymmetry of
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
.

Our compression is inherently asymmetric when
t\neq t^{\prime}
. In this case,
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}\neq\tau_{j^{\prime}j}
, and the slope conditions are likewise asymmetric, as reflected in the partition in Figure
1
(illustrated for
t<t^{\prime}
). This asymmetry stems from the fact that our compression is tailored to the asymmetric error metric
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
in our learning setting and, equivalently, to the weighted matrix operator norm
\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\cdot)\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|
. In contrast, the thresholding parameters and matrix compression schemes in
[
45
,
68
]
are symmetric.

5.

On the “second compression” in
[
122
,
45
]
.

Our compression uses only Proposition
3.2

(I)
. In addition,
[
122
,
45
]
derives sharper estimates for entries corresponding to wavelets with overlapping supports but widely separated scales, and then applies a second thresholding step. Concretely, for a function
f
on
\mathcal{M}
, let

\mathrm{sing\,supp}(f):=\{x\in\mathcal{M}:\ f\text{ is not smooth at }x\}
and let

S^{\prime}_{j,k}:=\mathrm{sing\,supp}(\psi_{j,k})\subset\mathcal{M}
be the singular support of
\psi_{j,k}
.
For
(j,k),(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{J}
such that
j^{\prime}>j
and
\mathrm{dist}(S^{\prime}_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\gtrsim 2^{-j^{\prime}}
,

[
45
, Theorem 6.3]
proves

|\mathbf{A}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}|\lesssim 2^{jn/2}2^{-j^{\prime}(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\,\mathrm{dist}(S^{\prime}_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{-(r+\widetilde{d})}.

This motivates a second thresholding step that retains only entries satisfying

\mathrm{dist}(S^{\prime}_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}},

where
\tau^{\prime}_{jj^{\prime}}
is a parameter distinct from
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
; see
[
68
, Definition 1]
and
[
45
, Section 7]
. We do not use this refinement here: in our setting it does not appear to improve the variance bound significantly, while discarding the regions
D_{1}\cup D_{2}
yields a simpler analysis and already provides strong variance estimates (at least for fixed
t,t^{\prime}
). ∎

4.2
Second Stage: Estimation

In this subsection, we construct an estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
for the sparse compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}.

The sparse support
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
will determine the structure of our regression-based estimator: the support specifies which matrix entries are actively regressed and which entries are set to zero and treated as bias.

We first observe that (
3.4
) can be written columnwisely. For any index
\lambda^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{J}
, the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th column of the matrix equation (
3.4
) reads

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}},

where
\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}},
and
\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
denote the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th columns of
\mathbf{F},\mathbf{A}
, and
\mathbf{W}
, respectively. For each
\lambda^{\prime}
, this yields an
infinite-dimensional linear regression problem
with response vector
\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
, design matrix
\mathbf{U}
, coefficient vector
\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
, and noise vector
\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
.
Based on (
4.5
), our strategy is to estimate the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}
columnwisely via regression, while carefully exploiting the sparse support structure of
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}
.

Note that the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th column of
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}\odot\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}
is supported on the finite set

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}:(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})}}\right\}.

However, directly regressing on this index set can induce a non-negligible
omitted-variable bias
, since the neglected components —though omitted from the approximation— still interact with the retained ones through the data; see Remark
4.7
for a detailed explanation. This bias is data-dependent and can be substantially larger than the deterministic truncation and compression errors incurred in the first stage. To address this issue,
a key idea of our regression estimator is as follows: for each column
\lambda^{\prime}
, we choose a slightly larger index set containing the support of
(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
as
regression support
, perform regression over this enlarged set, and then restrict the resulting estimator back to
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
. This
nested-support regression
strategy allows us to efficiently control the omitted-variable bias, keeping it at most of the same order as the truncation/compression error.

More precisely, let
\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},
and
\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
be three tuning parameters. Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B.2
ensures that the inclusion

(4.6)

\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})\subset\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})

holds provided that
\widetilde{J}\geq J,\widetilde{t}\geq t
, and
\widetilde{t}^{\prime}\geq t^{\prime}.
The values of the hyperparameters
\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
defining the enlarged regression support
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})\subset\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}
will be chosen so that the omitted-variable bias is of the same order as the truncation and compression errors.

Now, for each
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}
, define

\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}:(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})}}\right\}\subset\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}},\qquad\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c}:=\mathcal{J}\setminus\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}

and decompose the design matrix and coefficient vector accordingly:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}=[\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}},\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c}}],\quad\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}},\lambda^{\prime}}\\
\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c},\lambda^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}.

Here we use the convention that, for any subset
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{J}
,
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Lambda}\in R^{N\times\Lambda}
denotes the submatrix of
\mathbf{U}
formed by the columns indexed by
\Lambda
, and for any vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
denotes the subvector of
\mathbf{v}
with components indexed by
\Lambda
. The inclusion
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})\subset\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})
in (
4.6
) implies that, for each column
\lambda^{\prime}
, the vector
\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}},\lambda^{\prime}}
(strictly) contains all nonzero entries of
(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
that we aim to estimate.

Substituting (
4.7
) into (
4.5
), we obtain

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}},\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c}}\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c},\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}.

Assuming that
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
is invertible, we estimate
\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}},\lambda^{\prime}}
using the ordinary least squares estimator

(4.9)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}},

ignoring the contribution from
\mathbf{A}_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c},\lambda^{\prime}}
. We then concatenate the columnwise regression estimates, restrict the resulting matrix to
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
, and exploit the symmetry of
\mathbf{A}
to assemble the full estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
of the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}.
Using
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
, we define the final estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
of the unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
via (
4.1
). The complete definitions of
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
are given below.

Algorithm: Construction of the Estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}

Inputs:

•

Maximum wavelet scales
J
and
\widetilde{J}
; regularity parameters
t,t^{\prime},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
.

•

Compression support
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
, regression support
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})
.

•

Data matrices
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}
.

Output:
Estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:H^{t}(\mathcal{M})\to H^{-t^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M})
.

Step 1: Columnwise regression.

Let
\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}:(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})}}\right\}
. Compute the preliminary estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}\,\times\Lambda_{J}}
by concatenating columnwise regression estimates:

(4.10)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)})_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}:=\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\\
\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}},\qquad\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J},

where

(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}

and

\bm{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}\,\setminus\,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
.

Step 2: Restrict to the target sparsity pattern and use symmetry.

Define the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
of the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
entrywise by

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}&\text{if }(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})\text{ and }j\leq j^{\prime},\\[5.69054pt]
(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)})_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}&\text{if }(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})\text{ and }j>j^{\prime},\\[5.69054pt]
0&\text{if }(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime}).\end{cases}

Step 3: Operator construction.

Define the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
as

(4.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

Remark 4.4
(Interpretation of the construction of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).

Since the index set
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
is not symmetric, the compressed matrix

\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
(and hence our estimator) is not symmetric.
In
Step 1
, we construct a preliminary estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}

via column-wise regressions, without using the symmetry of the target matrix
\mathbf{A}
.
We now explain the motivation for the symmetrization procedure in
Step 2
,
which uses the lower-variance side of
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
to estimate its symmetric counterpart, thereby reducing the overall variance.

As will become clear from the variance analysis in Proposition
5.12

(see (
C.4.3
)), the standard deviation of an entry in the
(j,j^{\prime})
-block of

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
is of order
2^{jr_{1}-j^{\prime}r_{2}}/\sqrt{N}
, up to logarithmic factors.
Under Assumption
2.1
, we have

r_{1}>n/2+\max\{0,r\}
and
r_{2}>n/2
, and therefore, whenever
j<j^{\prime}
,

2^{jr_{1}-j^{\prime}r_{2}}<2^{j^{\prime}r_{1}-jr_{2}}.

Consequently, for
j<j^{\prime}
(i.e., for blocks
above
the diagonal
j=j^{\prime}
in the
(j,j^{\prime})
-plane in Figure
1
),
the entries of
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
in the
(j,j^{\prime})
-block have smaller variance than
their symmetric counterparts in the transposed
(j^{\prime},j)
-block (which lies
below
the diagonal).

This variance imbalance can be understood from the model

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{W}.

Roughly speaking, due to the regularity assumptions
r_{1},r_{2}>n/2
, the wavelet coefficients
of the input matrix
\mathbf{U}
and the noise matrix
\mathbf{W}
decay with the scale index. In particular,
when estimating the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th column of
\mathbf{A}
at a finer scale (large
j^{\prime}
), the effective noise level is smaller.
Moreover, the entries of
\mathbf{U}
at coarser scales are typically larger in magnitude, so
the corresponding regression has a stronger signal. Together, these effects yield smaller variance
for estimating
\mathbf{A}
above the diagonal than for estimating the entries below the diagonal.

Motivated by this observation, in
Step 2
we exploit the symmetry of the target matrix
\mathbf{A}
to transfer the more accurate
estimates (the lower-variance side, above the diagonal) to the opposite side (the higher-variance side, below the diagonal). Since our goal is to estimate the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
supported on
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
, when
t\leq t^{\prime}
,
Lemma
4.5
below guarantees that the reflection of the below-diagonal support
is contained in the above-diagonal support. This inclusion allows us to define the final estimator

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
in (
4.11
) by symmetrizing
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
using the
lower-variance entries, thereby reducing the overall variance compared with
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}
be the indicator matrix introduced in Definition
4.2
. If
t\leq t^{\prime}
, then for any pair of indices
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})=((j,k),(j^{\prime},k^{\prime}))
with
j>j^{\prime}
,
we have

(\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}=1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}=1.

Remark 4.6
(Effective data and computational cost).

Observe that, in the algorithm constructing the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
, we only use finite-dimensional data matrices

\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}},\qquad\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}},

rather than the full coefficient matrices
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{F}
. In our algorithm, we assume oracle access to the wavelet coefficients of the input–output data,
i.e., the quantities
(\langle u_{i},\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\rangle,\langle f_{i},\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)
.
This does not reduce the generality of the learning problem, since the (bi)orthogonal wavelet
transform is an invertible linear map. For algorithmic and implementation details of
wavelet–Galerkin methods, we refer the reader to
[
70
,
69
]
. In the following discussion on the computational cost of our learning algorithm, we ignore the cost of constructing the wavelets and computing these coefficients, and assume that
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{F}
are given.

We now discuss the computational cost of the algorithm. For a fixed column index
\lambda^{\prime}
, we
compute

(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\,\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}},

where

\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}:=\bigl\{\lambda\in\mathcal{J}:\;(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})\bigr\}.

Forming the Gram matrix
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}

and the cross term
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}
costs

\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}}}\right)
, and solving the resulting linear system
(e.g. via a Cholesky factorization) costs
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}}}\right)
.
Hence the per-column cost is

\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}}}\right).

Since we only perform column-wise regressions for indices
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}
to construct
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
, summing over all such columns
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}
yields a total computational cost of order

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\bigg(N\bigg(\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\bigg)+\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}\bigg),

which depends crucially on the sparsity pattern of the regression support
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})
. In Remark
5.5
, we provide a more explicit bound under a specific choice of
\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 4.7
(Structured infinite-dimensional linear regression).

In this remark, we interpret the column-wise model (
4.8
) and explain the structure behind the error analysis for the estimator (
4.9
). To streamline the discussion, we rewrite (
4.5
) as an abstract infinite-dimensional linear regression problem,

\displaystyle y=\mathbf{X}\theta+\xi,

where
y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the response vector,
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\mathcal{J}}
is a design matrix indexed by an infinite set
\mathcal{J}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}}
is an unknown coefficient vector, and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a noise vector. The goal is to recover
\theta
given
(\mathbf{X},y)
.

Suppose that the infinite-dimensional vector
\theta
exhibits structure through
a priori
information, suggesting that its dominant coordinates are contained in a finite index set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{J}
; denote the complement by
\Omega^{c}:=\mathcal{J}\backslash\Omega
. Decomposing both the design matrix and the parameter vector according to this partition yields

y=\mathbf{X}\theta+\xi=\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}\theta_{\Omega}+\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega^{c}}\theta_{\Omega^{c}}+\xi.

If
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}
is invertible, we define the estimator that regresses on
\Omega
and sets the remaining coordinates to zero,

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}\,:=\,\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\theta}_{\Omega}\\
\bm{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widehat{\theta}_{\Omega}:=(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}y.

A direct calculation gives

\displaystyle(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}y-\theta_{\Omega}=(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega^{c}}\theta_{\Omega^{c}}+(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\xi,

and therefore

(4.14)

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}-\theta=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega^{c}}\theta_{\Omega^{c}}+(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\xi\\
-\theta_{\Omega^{c}}\end{pmatrix}.

On the index set
\Omega^{c}
, the error is the
bias
term
-\theta_{\Omega^{c}}
, which is deterministic and independent of the design
\mathbf{X}
and the noise
\varepsilon
. Since
\Omega
is chosen to contain the significant entries of
\theta
, this term is expected to be small.

On the index set
\Omega
, the error decomposes into two terms. The first term

(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega^{c}}\theta_{\Omega^{c}}

is the
omitted-variable bias
(
\mathsf{OVB}
) term that depends on the covariance structure of the design. In classical finite-dimensional regression,
\mathsf{OVB}
arises when relevant covariates are omitted and their effect is partially attributed to included covariates; see, e.g.,
[
9
, Chapter 18]
and
[
146
, Chapter 3-3]
. Here,
\mathsf{OVB}
appears from a slightly different perspective: when
\theta
is infinite-dimensional, any finite-dimensional regression necessarily ignores a (presumably negligible) tail
\theta_{\Omega^{c}}
, and this tail can bias the estimate on
\Omega
when the columns of
\mathbf{X}
are correlated.

For random design with i.i.d. rows, one can make this precise. Let

\mathbf{C}:=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}]/N

denote the population covariance of each row of the design. Under standard regularity conditions, one typically has
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}/N\to\mathbf{C}_{\Omega,\Omega}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega^{c}}/N\to\mathbf{C}_{\Omega,\Omega^{c}}
as
N\to\infty
, and hence the
\mathsf{OVB}
term converges to

\mathbf{C}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}\mathbf{C}_{\Omega,\Omega^{c}}\,\theta_{\Omega^{c}}.

In general, this limit is not zero without additional structure on
\theta_{\Omega^{c}}
and/or
\mathbf{C}
(e.g. approximate orthogonality across the partition), so it contributes a genuine bias. Our nested-support strategy is designed to balance the two bias contributions: the deterministic bias
-\theta_{\Omega^{c}}
and the
\mathsf{OVB}
induced by
\theta_{\Omega^{c}}
through design correlations; see Proposition
5.11
in Subsection
5.2
.

The second term on the index set
\Omega
in (
4.14
),

(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\xi,

is the usual
variance
term. In the standard setting where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\xi}^{2}I_{N})
is independent of
\mathbf{X}
, one has
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\xi}}\right]=0
, and, conditional on
\mathbf{X}
,

(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\xi\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\sigma_{\xi}^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega})^{-1}}}\right).

In particular, under standard conditions ensuring
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\Omega}/N\to\mathbf{C}_{\Omega,\Omega}
, the typical size of this term decays at rate
N^{-1/2}
.

In our analysis of the matrix estimator (
4.11
) in Subsection
5.2
, the total error admits an analogous decomposition for each column
\lambda^{\prime}
. We note, however, that the regression support
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})
strictly contains the compression support
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
: this enlargement-restriction step in the nested-support strategy mitigates the omitted-variable bias, but introduces additional technical difficulty in the analysis. Moreover, since both the regression support set
\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and the noise level depend on
\lambda^{\prime}
, we first derive column-wise error bounds that exploit the associated sparsity pattern and noise scaling, and then aggregate these bounds across scales to control the overall matrix error under the weighted operator norm (
3.1
).
∎

5
Convergence Rates for Operator Learning

5.1
First Main Result

We are now ready to state and discuss our first main result:

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
3.1
hold. Let
t^{\prime}\geq t>r/2
. Consider the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(\{u_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N})
defined in Section
4
with parameters

(5.1)
