\displaystyle\widetilde{J}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r+\varepsilon_{1}}{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}\,J}}\right\rceil,\quad\widetilde{t}:=t^{\prime},\quad\widetilde{t}^{\prime}:=\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\},\quad J:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\log_{2}N}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}}\right\rceil,

where
\varepsilon_{1}:=n(t+t^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+t-r/2)
and

(5.2)

\displaystyle\rho:=2\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{-t-r_{2}+n/2}{\sigma-n/2+t-r/2},\,\frac{-t^{\prime}-r_{2}+n/2}{\sigma-n/2+t^{\prime}-r/2},\,\frac{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2}+n}{t+t^{\prime}+2\widetilde{d}},\,\frac{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2}}{t+t^{\prime}-r},0}}\right\}.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
N\gtrsim\log(1/\delta)
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\;\lesssim\;N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\,\sqrt{\log\!\Big(\frac{N}{\delta}\Big)}\,\log N.

Theorem
5.1
establishes a high-probability error bound of order
N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}
(up to logarithmic factors) for learning elliptic pseudo-differential operator from noisy data. It holds under general assumptions on the inputs and noise, together with an appropriate choice of wavelets. The implicit constant depends only on the parameters listed in Table
1
, which we treat as fixed throughout.

The rest of this subsection is organized as follows. Remark
5.2
discusses the assumption
t\leq t^{\prime}
and explains why it entails no loss of generality. Remark
5.3
provides a detailed discussion of the exponent
\rho
in (
5.2
), which governs the convergence rate; in particular, it highlights several qualitative features of the rate and shows that it is nearly optimal. Remark
5.4
shows that the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
has only
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\big)
nonzero coefficients, enabling fast numerical algorithms for downstream tasks. Remark
5.5
continues the computational-cost discussion in Remark
4.6
and shows that, under the parameter choice (
5.1
), the cost of constructing
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is nearly
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big(N^{1+\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\big)
, which is linear in the sample size
N
and linear in the learned-matrix dimension
2^{Jn}
. Remark
5.6
considers the noiseless setting and shows that our estimator achieves a super-algebraic convergence rate. Finally, Example
5.7
illustrates our results in the concrete setting of learning the Green’s function of elliptic PDEs, and compares both the convergence rate and computational cost with those in
[
121
]
.

Parameter

Description

N

Sample size: number of input–output data pairs
\{(u_{i},f_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}

n

Dimension of the physical domain (manifold
\mathcal{M}
)

r

Order of the pseudo-differential operator
\mathcal{A}

t,t^{\prime}

Input/output Sobolev index in the error norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}

r_{1},r_{2}

Regularity of the input function/noise in Assumption
2.1
:

u\in H^{(r_{1}-n/2)-},w\in H^{(r_{2}-n/2)-}

\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}

Regularity of the primal/dual wavelets

d,\widetilde{d}

Approximation order of the primal/dual wavelets

\sigma

Exponent controlling scale-separation decay of
\mathbf{A}

(Proposition
3.2

(I)
(ii); Assumption
3.1
(iii))

Table 1:
Summary of key parameters.

Remark 5.2
(On the assumption
t\leq t^{\prime}
).

In Theorem
5.1
, we assumed that
t\leq t^{\prime}
only to simplify the presentation, which entails no loss of generality. Indeed, when
t\geq t^{\prime}
, we may take the adjoint
(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})^{*}
of the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
defined in (
4.12
) as our estimator. Since
\mathcal{A}
is self-adjoint,

(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})^{*}-\mathcal{A}=(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})^{*}.

Moreover, by the dual characterization of Sobolev norms and the definition of the adjoint,

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})^{*}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\|(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})^{*}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|w\|_{H^{t}}\leq 1,\|v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\leq 1}|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})^{*}w,\,v\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|w\|_{H^{t}}\leq 1,\|v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\leq 1}|\langle w,\,(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})v\rangle|=\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}\to H^{-t}}.

Therefore, applying Theorem
5.1
with
t
and
t^{\prime}
exchanged yields an upper bound for

\|(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})^{*}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
. Since the exponent
\rho
in (
5.2
) is symmetric in
(t,t^{\prime})
, the resulting convergence rate coincides with that in the regime
t\leq t^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
(Discussion on the convergence rate).

We make several remarks on the exponent
\rho
in (
5.2
), which governs the convergence rate.

1.

Dependence on
t,t^{\prime}
.

The indices
t,t^{\prime}
quantify the strength of the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
used to measure the estimation
error. Roughly speaking,
larger

t,t^{\prime}
correspond to a
weaker
norm, hence a less stringent error metric. When
t
and
t^{\prime}
are sufficiently large relative to
r_{1},r_{2},n
, namely when

(5.3)

\displaystyle\min\{t,t^{\prime}\}\geq-r_{2}+\frac{n}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad t+t^{\prime}\geq r_{1}-r_{2}+n,

we have
\rho=0
, and thus attain the parametric rate
N^{-1/2}
(up to logarithmic factors). Indeed, under (
5.3
) the effective variance grows at most polynomially in

J
(as will be reflected in our error analysis), so the bias–variance tradeoff takes the form

2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\frac{\mathrm{poly}(J)}{\sqrt{N}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad N^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{polylog}(N).

When (
5.3
) fails, the variance grows exponentially in
J
, leading to a
genuinely nonparametric rate
(\rho>0)
:

2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\frac{2^{J\rho(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2}\mathrm{poly}(J)}{\sqrt{N}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\,\mathrm{polylog}(N).

2.

Dependence on
n,r,r_{1},r_{2}
.

Recall that
n
is the dimension of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
, and
r
is the order of the PDO
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
. The parameters
r_{1}
and
r_{2}

control the regularity of the input and the noise:
u\in H^{(r_{1}-n/2)-}
and
w\in H^{(r_{2}-n/2)-}
almost surely; see Remark
2.2
. From (
5.2
),

\rho
is increasing in
n,r,r_{1}
and decreasing in
r_{2}
. Consequently, a lower-dimensional
manifold (smaller
n
), a lower-order operator (smaller
r
), a rougher input (smaller
r_{1}
), and/or a smoother noise (larger
r_{2}
) lead to a smaller
\rho
, and hence a faster convergence rate

N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}
.

3.

Dependence on
\sigma,\widetilde{d}
.

The parameters
\sigma
and
\widetilde{d}
depend on the choice of biorthogonal wavelets.
The parameter
\sigma
quantifies the off-diagonal decay of the entries of
\mathbf{A}
across scale
separation (especially for wavelets with overlapping supports); see
Proposition
3.2

(I)
(ii). It is required to satisfy
Assumption
3.1
(iii): its lower bound is fixed, while its admissible upper
bound increases with the wavelet regularity and approximation parameters

(\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{d})
. In particular, by choosing smoother wavelets with higher approximation order, one can take
\sigma
larger (indeed,
\sigma
may scale
proportionally to
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{d}
). When (
5.3
) fails, at least one of the first three terms in
(
5.2
) is strictly positive. In this case, increasing
\sigma
and/or
\widetilde{d}
reduces
\rho
, and therefore improves the convergence rate
N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}
.

4.

Intrinsic convergence rate.

In an idealized regime where
\sigma
and
\widetilde{d}
are taken sufficiently large (formally,

\sigma,\widetilde{d}\to\infty
), the first three contributions in (
5.2
) vanish and
\rho

reduces to

\rho=2\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2}}{t+t^{\prime}-r},\,0}}\right\}.

This exponent is independent of the wavelet choice and determines an intrinsic convergence rate governed by
(t,t^{\prime},r_{1},r_{2},r)
. Intuitively, increasing
\sigma
and
\widetilde{d}
allows one to fully exploit the
off-diagonal smoothness of
\mathcal{A}
, yet the rate is still governed by the interplay between the error metric
(t,t^{\prime})
, the input function regularity
r_{1}
, and the noise regularity
r_{2}
. In this regime, the bias–variance tradeoff takes the form

(5.4)

\displaystyle 2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\frac{2^{J\max\{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2},\,0\}}}{\sqrt{N}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad N^{-\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{2(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)},\,\frac{1}{2}}}\right\}}.

The quantity
r_{1}-r_{2}-r
may be interpreted as an
effective smoothness
, i.e., the gap
between the regularity of the signal
\mathcal{A}u\in H^{(r_{1}-r-n/2)-}
and that of the noise

w\in H^{(r_{2}-n/2)-}
. Larger values of
r_{1}-r_{2}-r
(smoother
\mathcal{A}u
and/or rougher noise
w
) make
the problem harder and lead to slower learning rates. In particular, when
r_{1}-r_{2}>t+t^{\prime}
(equivalently,

r_{1}-r_{2}-r>t+t^{\prime}-r
), we have

\rho=\frac{2(-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2})}{t+t^{\prime}-r},\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}=N^{-\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{2(r_{1}-r_{2}-r)}}.

Finally, we remark that the factor

2^{J\max\{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2},0\}}/\sqrt{N}
corresponds to the standard deviation of estimating a single
entry in the
(0,0)
and
(J,J)
blocks of the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
. To see this, consider the simplest idealized setting in which
\mathbf{A}
is exactly diagonal —an assumption that typically fails for a general PDO
\mathcal{A}
in wavelet coordinates. In this case, the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th column
equation in (
4.5
) reduces to

\mathbf{F}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{A}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}},

where
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,(\sigma^{U}_{\lambda^{\prime}})^{2}I_{N}\bigr)
and

\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,(\sigma^{W}_{\lambda^{\prime}})^{2}I_{N}\bigr)
are independent, with

\sigma^{U}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\asymp 2^{-j^{\prime}r_{1}}
and
\sigma^{W}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\asymp 2^{-j^{\prime}r_{2}}
by
Proposition
3.2

(II)
(ii)-(iii). Thus, recovering the
\lambda^{\prime}
-th column of
\mathbf{A}
reduces to estimating the single scalar parameter
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
.

In this Gaussian location model, the scalar least squares estimator (equivalently, the maximum
likelihood estimator) satisfies

|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}-\mathbf{A}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}|\asymp\frac{\sigma^{W}_{\lambda^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{N}\,\sigma^{U}_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\asymp\frac{2^{j^{\prime}(r_{1}-r_{2})}}{\sqrt{N}},

where
\sigma^{U}_{\lambda^{\prime}}/\sigma^{W}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is the signal-to-noise ratio. Moreover, this rate
is minimax optimal (e.g., by a direct Le Cam’s two-point argument). Finally, the norm

\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
contributes the additional weight
2^{-j^{\prime}(t+t^{\prime})}
, so the

weighted
standard deviation scales as

\frac{2^{j^{\prime}(-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2})}}{\sqrt{N}}.

This equals
1/\sqrt{N}
when
j^{\prime}=0
, and equals
2^{J(-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2})}/\sqrt{N}
when
j^{\prime}=J
.
This explains the appearance of
2^{J\max\{-t-t^{\prime}+r_{1}-r_{2},0\}}/\sqrt{N}
in
(
5.4
), and suggests that, without further assumptions on the operator or on the covariance
structure of the input function and the noise, the rate in (
5.4
) cannot be improved. ∎

Remark 5.4
(Sparsity of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
:
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
nonzero coefficients).

Our estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is optimally sparse in wavelet coordinates, in the sense that its matrix representation

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
has only
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
nonzero entries, which is linear in
|\Lambda_{J}|\asymp 2^{Jn}
. Indeed,
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
is supported on
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
by construction, and Proposition
C.2
guarantees that

\mathrm{nnz}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}})=\mathrm{nnz}\big(\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}\big)\lesssim 2^{Jn}
.
Under the bias–variance optimal choice of
J
in (
5.1
), we have
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\big)
nonzero coefficients in
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
.
∎

Remark 5.5
(Computational cost: nearly
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})
).

In this remark, we continue the discussion of computational cost from Remark
4.6
and give a more precise bound of this cost under our choice of parameters in (
5.1
).
Recall from (
4.13
) that the computational cost of our estimator is determined by the sparsity structure of the regression support
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime})
:

\mathcal{O}\bigg(N\bigg(\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\bigg)+\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}\bigg).

To simplify notation, let
\widetilde{J}:=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})J
and define

\nu:=\frac{n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}.

By the column-wise support bound in Proposition
C.2
, for
\lambda^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{J}
,

|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|\;\lesssim\;\widetilde{J}+2^{\nu(\widetilde{J}-j^{\prime})}.

For
p\in\{2,3\}
, using
(x+y)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(x^{p}+y^{p})
(for
x,y>0
) and
|\nabla_{j^{\prime}}|\asymp 2^{j^{\prime}n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\nabla_{j^{\prime}}}\Big(\widetilde{J}+2^{\nu(\widetilde{J}-j^{\prime})}\Big)^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}2^{j^{\prime}n}\Big(\widetilde{J}^{p}+2^{p\nu(\widetilde{J}-j^{\prime})}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\widetilde{J}^{p}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}2^{j^{\prime}n}\;+\;2^{p\nu\widetilde{J}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}2^{(n-p\nu)j^{\prime}}.

Assume
\nu<n/p
(which holds for
\sigma
large enough). Then the geometric sum satisfies

\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}2^{(n-p\nu)j^{\prime}}\lesssim 2^{(n-p\nu)J}
, and hence

\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{p}\lesssim J^{p}2^{Jn}+2^{Jn}\,2^{p\nu(\widetilde{J}-J)}\lesssim 2^{Jn\Big(1+\frac{p\varepsilon^{\prime}(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}\Big)}.

In particular,

\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\lesssim 2^{Jn\Big(1+\frac{2\varepsilon^{\prime}(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}\Big)},\qquad\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}\lesssim 2^{Jn\Big(1+\frac{3\varepsilon^{\prime}(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}\Big)}.

Therefore, the total computational cost satisfies

(5.5)

\displaystyle N\bigg(\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\bigg)+\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}|\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}|^{3}\lesssim N2^{Jn(1+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}+2^{Jn\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}}\right)}\lesssim N2^{Jn(1+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})},

provided that

N\gtrsim 2^{Jn\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}/2},\qquad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}:=\frac{2\varepsilon^{\prime}(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}>0.

A sufficient (stronger) condition is

N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}.

This holds under our choice
J=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\log_{2}N}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}}\right\rceil
in (
5.1
); see (
5.13
).

Since
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\to 0
as
\sigma\to\infty
(i.e., as we choose smoother biorthogonal wavelets), the computational cost (
5.5
) can be made arbitrarily close to
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})
, that is, linear in the sample size
N
and, up to constants, linear in the number of wavelet indices
|\Lambda_{J}|\asymp 2^{Jn}
. Recall that the estimator in (
4.11
) satisfies

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
.
In contrast, any direct estimator that explicitly forms
\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{J}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Lambda_{J}}

already incurs a cost of
\mathcal{O}(N2^{2Jn})
, i.e., quadratic in
|\Lambda_{J}|
.
Furthermore, under the choice of
J
in (
5.1
), which optimally balances truncation,
compression, and estimation errors, we obtain

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{1+\frac{n}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}}}\right).

Finally, note that the dominant contribution to the above bounds comes from columns with
j^{\prime}=J
. Indeed, when
j^{\prime}=J
and
k^{\prime}\in\nabla_{J}
, one expects the corresponding column of the compressed matrix

\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
to contain only
\mathcal{O}(1)
significant entries. We conjecture that, with a sharper choice of the enlarged regression supports, one can improve the bound (
5.5
) and achieve the

exact
optimal computational cost
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})
, while keeping the statistical convergence rate established in Theorem
5.1
; we leave this question for future work.
∎

Remark 5.6
(Noiseless setting: super-algebraic convergence rate).

We can adapt the analysis of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
to the noiseless setting in a straightforward way. Assume that the data pairs
\{u_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfy

(5.7)

\displaystyle f_{i}=\mathcal{A}u_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,

so that in the wavelet–matrix formulation

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{A}.

Under Assumption
2.1
(without (iii)) and Assumption
3.1
, we use the same estimator

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
as in (
4.12
). Following the same argument as in the noisy case yields

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB},

where the variance term
\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}
is absent. By Propositions
5.9
,
5.10
, and

5.11
, if

\displaystyle N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}+\log(1/\delta),

then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}\lesssim J\,2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}.

Consequently, we may choose
\widetilde{J}
by saturating the sample size constraint,

N\asymp 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\widetilde{J}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}{n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}\,\log_{2}N}}\right\rceil,

and then set, using the scaling relation between
J
and
\widetilde{J}
in (
5.1
),

(5.9)

\displaystyle J=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}{t+t^{\prime}-r+\varepsilon_{1}}\,\widetilde{J}}}\right\rceil=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}{t+t^{\prime}-r+\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\frac{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}{n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}\,\log_{2}N}}\right\rceil.

With this choice,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim J\,2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\,\lesssim_{\,q}\,N^{-q}\log N,

where

(5.11)

\displaystyle q:=(t+t^{\prime}-r)\cdot\frac{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}{t+t^{\prime}-r+\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\frac{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}{n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)}.

In particular,
q
grows linearly with
\sigma
(and
\sigma
can grow linearly with
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{d}
), so
q
can be made arbitrarily large by choosing sufficiently smooth biorthogonal wavelets with sufficiently high approximation orders. Equivalently, for any
q_{0}>0
, one can take the wavelet so that
q\geq q_{0}
, which yields the high-probability bound

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\;\lesssim_{\,q_{0}}\;N^{-q_{0}}\log N.

Thus, in the noiseless setting, the error decays super-algebraically in
N
(up to a logarithmic factor) as the wavelet smoothness increases.

Moreover, under the choice of
J
in (
5.9
), the estimated matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
has size
|\Lambda_{J}|\asymp 2^{Jn}\asymp N^{\frac{qn}{t+t^{\prime}-r}},

and
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
contains
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{qn}{t+t^{\prime}-r}}\big)
nonzero wavelet coefficients. Moreover, the computational cost of constructing the estimator is nearly
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big(N^{1+\frac{qn}{t+t^{\prime}-r}}\big)
, where
q
is as in (
5.11
).
∎

Example 5.7

(Learning Green’s function of a uniformly elliptic PDE)
.

In the discussion above, the size of the learned matrix, the sparsity level (i.e., the number of nonzero coefficients), and the computational cost all depend on the sample size
N
. This reflects a standard feature of nonparametric statistics: the effective dimension of the learning problem grows with the amount of data. Since our bounds depend on multiple parameters, we illustrate their implications below through a concrete example.

Consider the (suitably normalized) solution operator (Green’s function) associated with a uniformly elliptic PDE of order
2s
, where
s>0
. In this setting, the solution operator belongs to
\mathsf{OPS}^{-2s}(\mathcal{M})
, i.e.,
r=-2s
. Recall that we work on an
n
-dimensional smooth closed manifold; extensions to more general domains with appropriate boundary conditions are natural. To simplify the discussion, we consider the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{M})}
, i.e., we set
t=t^{\prime}=0
.

•

Noisy setting.
Since
r=-2s
,
t=t^{\prime}=0
, and
r_{2}>n/2
, the parameter
\rho
in (
5.2
) becomes

\rho=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}+n}{\widetilde{d}},\,\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{s},0}}\right\}.

By Theorem
5.1
and Remarks
5.4
and
5.5
, the convergence rate, the size of the matrix estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
and its number of nonzero entries (nnz), and the computational cost of constructing the estimator scale with
N
as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Convergence rate:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\log^{\frac{3}{2}}(N)\big).

\displaystyle\text{Size and nnz of $\widehat{\mathbf{A}}$:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{n}{2s(2+\rho)}}\big).

\displaystyle\text{Computational cost:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{1+\frac{n}{2s(2+\rho)}}\big).

Therefore, to achieve a target accuracy tolerance
\epsilon
under
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
in the noisy setting, i.e., to ensure
N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\log^{\frac{3}{2}}N\asymp\epsilon
, it suffices to take

\displaystyle\text{\#input--output noisy data pairs:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-(2+\rho)}\log^{\frac{3(2+\rho)}{2}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

This in turn implies:

\displaystyle\text{Size and nnz of $\widehat{\mathbf{A}}$:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{3n}{4s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

\displaystyle\text{Computational cost:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-(2+\rho)-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{6s(2+\rho)+3n}{4s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

•

Noiseless setting.

When
r=-2s
and
t=t^{\prime}=0
, the exponent
q
in (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) becomes

q=\frac{2s}{1+\frac{n}{\sigma-n/2+s}}\cdot\frac{\sigma-n/2+r_{1}+s}{n(r_{1}+2s)}.

By Remark
5.6
, we now have:

\displaystyle\text{Convergence rate:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{-q}\log N\big).

\displaystyle\text{Size and nnz of $\widehat{\mathbf{A}}$:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{\frac{qn}{2s}}\big).

\displaystyle\text{Computational cost:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(N^{1+\frac{qn}{2s}}\big).

Therefore, to achieve a target accuracy tolerance
\epsilon
under
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
in the noiseless setting, i.e., to ensure
N^{-q}\log N\asymp\epsilon
, it suffices to take

\displaystyle\text{\#input--output noiseless data pairs:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q}}\log^{\frac{1}{q}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

This in turn implies:

\displaystyle\text{Size and nnz of $\widehat{\mathbf{A}}$:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{n}{2s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

\displaystyle\text{Computational cost:}\quad\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q}-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{1}{q}+\frac{n}{2s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big).

We compare the above results with
[
121
]
, which studies recovery of a discretized (finite-dimensional) Green’s matrix from carefully designed, noiseless vector measurements. Their method exploits approximate sparsity in a Cholesky factorization of solution operators for elliptic PDEs of order
2s
, where
s
is a positive integer, and then approximates the continuous Green’s function via piecewise constant or piecewise affine interpolation. In particular, to achieve accuracy
\epsilon
for recovering the continuous Green’s function under the
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
norm,
[
121
]
requires computational cost
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-n}\log^{2n+2}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
using
\mathcal{O}\big(\log^{n+1}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
noiseless, carefully chosen data pairs; see
[
121
, Theorem 3.5]
. The leading
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-n})
dependence arises from the final low-order interpolation step that uses the recovered discrete Green’s matrix to approximate the continuous Green’s function; consequently, the PDE order
2s
(and hence the strength of elliptic smoothing) does not appear in the leading
\epsilon
-exponent.

By contrast, our noiseless guarantees show that with generic Gaussian input data, achieving accuracy
\epsilon
requires
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q}}\log^{\frac{1}{q}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
data pairs and the computational cost is of order
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{q}-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{1}{q}+\frac{n}{2s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
. By choosing smoother wavelets with higher approximation order, the exponent
q
can be made large, so the required number of data pairs can grow arbitrarily slower than any fixed polynomial in
\epsilon^{-1}
. In the large-
q
regime, our computational cost approaches
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{n}{2s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
, which (when
s>1/2
) improves the
\epsilon
-dependence relative to
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-n}\log^{2n+2}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
in
[
121
, Theorem 3.5]
. We explain why the computational cost
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\log^{\frac{n}{2s}}(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
is optimal up to logarithmic factors in this setting. To achieve accuracy
\epsilon
in
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
, one can at most discard information beyond the scale
J
such that
2^{-2Js}\asymp\epsilon
, since
\mathcal{O}(2^{-2Js})
is the unavoidable bias incurred by ignoring finer scales. In
n
dimensions, the number of degrees of freedom up to scale
J
is of order
\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})
, and each degree of freedom must be learned at a cost of at least
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Therefore, any method must have total cost at least

\mathcal{O}(2^{Jn})=\mathcal{O}\big((2^{-2Js})^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\big)=\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\big),

matching our complexity bound up to logarithmic factors.

In addition, our framework accommodates noisy observations and generic Gaussian inputs, works under general Sobolev-to-Sobolev operator norms, and applies to fractional elliptic PDEs (where the underlying differential operator is nonlocal) —settings not covered by
[
121
]
. In the noisy case, achieving
\epsilon
accuracy entails computational cost of order (up to logarithmic factors)

\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-(2+\rho)-\frac{n}{2s}}\big),\quad\rho=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}+n}{\widetilde{d}},\,\frac{r_{1}-r_{2}}{s},0}}\right\},

which is worse by a factor of roughly
\epsilon^{-(2+\rho)}
compared with our noiseless scaling. The
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-2})
factor is unavoidable in general: it reflects the central-limit-theorem scaling induced by additive noise. In particular, to estimate each degree of freedom to accuracy
\epsilon
from noisy samples, one typically needs
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-2})
observations. The factor
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-\frac{n}{2s}}\big)
accounts for the number of degrees of freedom that must be learned (up to the accuracy-determining scale), as discussed in the previous paragraph. To our knowledge, this provides the first explicit and nearly optimal statistical accuracy–computational cost tradeoff for learning Green’s functions of elliptic PDEs from noisy data.
∎

5.2
Proof of Theorem
5.1

This subsection proves Theorem
5.1
. We first decompose the total error into three contributions: truncation, compression, and estimation. The truncation error is the bias from restricting the infinite-dimensional matrix
\mathbf{A}
to its finite-resolution version
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}
, while the compression error is the deterministic approximation error from sparsifying
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}
to
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
; see Subsection
4.1
. Both terms are data-independent and are controlled by Propositions
5.9
and
5.10
, respectively.
The estimation error is the only data-dependent term and quantifies the statistical uncertainty in estimating the compressed matrix
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}
. Lemma
5.8
further decomposes it into an omitted-variable bias term (still random) and a variance term, which are bounded in Propositions
5.11
and
5.12
, respectively. Proofs of the lemma and propositions are deferred to Appendices
C.2
,
C.3
, and
C.4
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{D}^{s}_{\Lambda_{J}}:=(\mathbf{D}^{s}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
denote the diagonal weight matrix restricted to
\Lambda_{J}
. For any index set
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{J}
, let
P_{\Lambda}:\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})\to\ell^{2}(\Lambda)
be the coordinate projection and
I_{\Lambda}:=P_{\Lambda}^{*}
be its adjoint (the zero-padding injection).
For our matrix estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
in (
4.11
), and the
a priori
truncation matrix
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
in Definition
4.1
, define their zero-padded extensions

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\uparrow}:=I_{\Lambda_{J}}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}P_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}},\qquad\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow}:=I_{\Lambda_{J}}\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}P_{\Lambda_{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}}.

We begin with the operator-norm equivalence (
3.1
),

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\asymp\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|.

Adding and subtracting
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow}
yields

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\asymp\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|+\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|

(5.12)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|}_{=:\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}}+\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{-t}\|.

Decomposing the second term gives

\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{-t}\|\leq\underbrace{\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|}_{=:\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}}+\underbrace{\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|}_{=:\mathsf{Error\text{-}Estimation}}.

By Lemma
5.8
, the estimation error further decomposes into the omitted–variable bias
and the variance contributions:

\mathsf{Error\text{-}Estimation}=\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|\leq\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}.

Propositions
5.9
and
5.10
give the deterministic bounds

\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}\lesssim 2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)},\qquad\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}\lesssim J2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}.

Moreover, Propositions
5.11
and
5.12
show that, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if

(5.13)

\displaystyle N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}+\log(1/\delta),

then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}\lesssim J2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)},\qquad\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{J+\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot J\cdot 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2},

where
\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
are defined in (
5.1
) and
\rho
is defined in (
5.2
).

Thus, under (
5.13
), with probability at least
1-\delta
we have

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim J2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\sqrt{\frac{J+\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot J\cdot 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2}.

To balance the bias and variance terms, choose

\displaystyle J:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\log_{2}N}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}}\right\rceil.

Then

2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\asymp N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}},\qquad 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2}\asymp N^{\frac{\rho}{2(2+\rho)}},

and hence

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\;\lesssim\;N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\,\sqrt{\log\!\Big(\frac{N}{\delta}\Big)}\,\log N.

It remains to verify the sample-size condition (
5.13
) under this choice of
J
. Since
J\asymp\frac{\log_{2}N}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}
, we have

N=2^{(2+\rho)J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)},

provided

(2+\rho)J(t+t^{\prime}-r)>\widetilde{J}n\frac{\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r}{\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2}.

By the definition (
5.1
) of
\widetilde{J},\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
,
this is equivalent to

(2+\rho)J(t+t^{\prime}-r)>\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r+\varepsilon_{1}}{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}\,Jn\,\frac{t^{\prime}+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r}{\sigma-n/2+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r/2},

where
\varepsilon_{1}=n(t+t^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+t-r/2)
. Since
\rho\geq 0
, it suffices that

2>\frac{n+\frac{n^{2}}{\sigma-n/2+t-r/2}}{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}\,\cdot\frac{t^{\prime}+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r}{\sigma-n/2+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r/2}.

Noting that
\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}\geq r_{1}>n/2+\max\{0,r\}\geq n/2+r/2
, we see that a sufficient condition for the above inequality is

\sigma>\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{3n}{2}-t+\frac{r}{2},\ \frac{t^{\prime}+\max\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}-r}{\min\{t^{\prime},r_{1}\}+t-r}n}}\right\}.

This is guaranteed by Assumption
3.1
(v), and thus the sample-size condition (
5.13
) holds for all sufficiently large
N
. The claimed bound follows.
∎

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we invoked Lemma
5.8
to derive the error decomposition, and Propositions
5.9
,
5.10
,
5.11
, and
5.12
to bound, respectively, the truncation error, the compression error, the omitted-variable bias, and the variance term. The proofs of these results are provided in Appendices
C.2
,
C.3
, and
C.4
.

Lemma 5.8

(Decomposition of
\mathsf{Error\text{-}Estimation}
)
.

Under the setting and assumptions of Theorem
5.1
, the estimation error
\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|
admits the decomposition:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\varepsilon})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|\leq\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var},

where the omitted-variable bias and variance contributions are

\displaystyle\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\mathrm{up}}\odot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{OVB}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}}}\right\|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(((\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\mathrm{low}})^{\top}\odot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{OVB}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}}}\right\|,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\mathrm{up}}\odot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}}}\right\|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(((\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})})_{\mathrm{low}})^{\top}\odot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}}}\right\|,

with

\displaystyle\mathsf{OVB}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{c}}\bf\mathbf{A}_{\Omega^{c}_{\lambda^{\prime}},\lambda^{\prime}}}}\right)e_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}\,\times\Lambda_{J}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(E_{\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}})^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\cdot,\Omega_{\lambda^{\prime}}}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{\cdot,\lambda^{\prime}}}}\right)e_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{\widetilde{J}}\,\times\Lambda_{J}}.

Here
\mathsf{OVB}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}
and
\mathsf{Var}_{\Lambda_{J},\cdot}
denote the restrictions of
\mathsf{OVB}
and
\mathsf{Var}
to rows indexed by
\Lambda_{J}
, and
\mathbf{M}_{(J,t,t^{\prime})}
is the indicator matrix associated with
\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
defined in (
4.4
).

Proposition 5.9

(Bound for
\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}
)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
(i) and Assumption
3.1
hold. For
t,t^{\prime}\geq r/2
,

\mathsf{Error\text{-}Truncation}=\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{\uparrow}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|\lesssim 2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}.

Proposition 5.10

(Bound for
\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}
)
.

Suppose Assumption

2.1
(i) and Assumption
3.1
hold. For
\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}
defined in Definition
4.2
and
t_{\ell},t_{r}\in[r/2,\gamma)
,

\|\mathbf{D}^{-t_{l}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}^{-t_{r}}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|\lesssim J2^{-J\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\{t^{\prime},t_{l}\}+\min\{t,t_{r}\}-r}}\right)}.

In particular, for
t,t^{\prime}\geq r/2
,

\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}=\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\mathbf{A}^{\varepsilon}_{\Lambda_{J}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|\lesssim J\,2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}.

Proposition 5.11

(Bound for
\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}
)
.

Under the setting and assumptions of Theorem
5.1
, the following holds. For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if

N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}+\log(1/\delta),

then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}\lesssim J\,2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}.

Proposition 5.12

(Bound for
\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}
)
.

Under the setting and assumptions of Theorem
5.1
, the following holds. For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if

N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}+\log(1/\delta),

then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{J+\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot J\cdot 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2},

where
\rho
is the exponent defined in (
5.2
).

6
Convergence Rates for Data-Driven PDE Solver

In the classical wavelet–Galerkin framework, an effective matrix compression strategy is typically expected to preserve the optimal convergence order of the underlying Galerkin scheme. In that setting, no data are involved and the operator
\mathcal{A}
is fully known. In contrast, in our setting the operator
\mathcal{A}
is unknown and must be learned from data. This raises the question of whether the learned operator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
provides an effective PDE solver for previously unseen right-hand side
f.

In this section, we study the convergence rate of the numerical solution obtained by plugging the learned sparse operator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
into the elliptic pseudo-differential equation.
We show that this rate inherits the statistical accuracy of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and is analogous to that of the classical wavelet–Galerkin method, while exhibiting new features specific to the data-driven setting —most notably that the maximal wavelet scale is chosen as a function of the sample size in the learning stage. This analysis highlights the usefulness of operator learning for downstream PDE solvers.

Subsection
6.1
presents the error bound and Subsection
6.2
contains its proof.

6.1
Second Main Result

Before presenting the result, we introduce a slight modification of the estimator in Theorem
5.1
so that the resulting matrix estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
satisfies the
V_{J}
ellipticity condition, which will be crucial in establishing the convergence rate of the numerical solution; see
[
45
]
.

Recall that in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, combining Proposition
5.11
,
5.12
, and
5.10
, we established that the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}}
satisfies the following bound. Let
\delta\in(0,1)
. If

N\gtrsim 2^{\widetilde{J}n(\widetilde{t}+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r)/(\sigma-n/2+\widetilde{t}^{\prime}-r/2)}+\log(1/\delta),

then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{Error\text{-}OVB}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Var}+\mathsf{Error\text{-}Compression}

\displaystyle\lesssim J\,2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\sqrt{\frac{J+\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot J\cdot 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2},

where
\rho
is the exponent defined in (
5.2
).

We now claim that, after slightly modifying the estimator in Theorem
5.1
and adapting the analysis, the following refined bound can be attained: there exists some constant
\kappa>0
such that, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J}}\|\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-J(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot(J/\varepsilon)^{\kappa}\cdot 2^{\rho J(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2}.

Here
\varepsilon>0
is a tunable hyperparameter, and
\rho
is as in (
5.2
).
Choosing
J=J_{*}
such that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\asymp\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot(J_{*}/\varepsilon)^{\kappa}\cdot 2^{\rho J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2},

we therefore conclude that, if
N\gtrsim\log(1/\delta)
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}^{-t}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{N}}\cdot(J_{*}/\varepsilon)^{\kappa}\cdot 2^{\rho J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)/2}

\displaystyle\asymp\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}

(6.3)

\displaystyle\asymp_{\mathrm{polylog}}N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}.

Here we use the notation
\asymp_{\mathrm{polylog}}

to hide poly-logarithmic factors in
N
and
1/\delta
.

Remark 6.1
(Modification for the enhanced bound (
6.1
)).

We briefly explain how to modify the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
in order to obtain the bound (
6.1
).
We introduce a new support set
\mathrm{supp}^{\mathrm{new}}(J,t,t^{\prime})
by modifying both the thresholding parameters
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
and the slope conditions in the definition of

\mathrm{supp}(J,t,t^{\prime})
in (
4.2
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}^{\mathrm{new}}(J,t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{J}\times\Lambda_{J}:\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\leq\tau^{\mathrm{new}}_{jj^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad j\leq\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2}}J+\frac{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}-(t-\frac{r}{2})}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2}}j^{\prime}+\frac{\log_{2}(J/\varepsilon)}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2}},

(6.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad j^{\prime}\leq\frac{t+t^{\prime}-r}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t-\frac{r}{2}}J+\frac{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}-(t^{\prime}-\frac{r}{2})}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t-\frac{r}{2}}j+\frac{\log_{2}(J/\varepsilon)}{\sigma-\frac{n}{2}+t-\frac{r}{2}}\bigg\},

where the new thresholding parameter is chosen as

(6.5)

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathrm{new}}_{jj^{\prime}}\asymp\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{2^{-\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}},2^{\frac{J(t+t^{\prime}-r)-jt^{\prime}-j^{\prime}t-(j+j^{\prime})\widetilde{d}+\log_{2}(J/\varepsilon)}{2\widetilde{d}+r}}}}\right\}.

We then define
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
using this updated support
\mathrm{supp}^{\mathrm{new}}(J,t,t^{\prime})
. We defer the proof of (
6.1
) for this modified estimator to
Appendix
D
.
∎

The next theorem quantifies the convergence rate of the PDE solution obtained by replacing
\mathcal{A}
with the learned sparse operator satisfying (
6.1
).

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.1
and
3.1
hold, and assume that the operator
\mathcal{A}
is unknown. Let
f\in H^{-r/2}(\mathcal{M})
, and let
u
be the exact solution of
\mathcal{A}u=f
. Let
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J_{*}}\times\Lambda_{J_{*}}}
be the data-driven sparse estimator satisfying
(
6.1
)
, and let
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}
solve

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}:=\bigl(\langle f,\psi_{\lambda}\rangle\bigr)_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{J_{*}}}

denotes the vector of wavelet coefficients of
f
truncated to scales up to
J_{*}
. Define

\widehat{u}:=\widehat{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\Psi=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\widehat{u}_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, for any
\alpha\in[0,t^{\prime}-r/2]
,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t+\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\asymp_{\mathrm{polylog}}N^{-\frac{t+\alpha-r/2}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\|u\|_{H^{t}},

where
\rho
is the exponent defined in (
5.2
).

Remark 6.3
(Discussion on Theorem
6.2
).

In Theorem
6.2
, choosing
\alpha=t^{\prime}-r/2
yields the fastest convergence rate

\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\asymp_{\mathrm{polylog}}N^{-\frac{1}{2+\rho}}\|u\|_{H^{t}}.

This matches the convergence rate for learning the unknown operator
\mathcal{A}
established in Theorem
5.1
. Thus, the data-driven PDE solver inherits the statistical accuracy of the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

It is instructive to compare this with the classical deterministic theory of compressed wavelet Galerkin schemes
[
45
]
. When the operator
\mathcal{A}
(or its wavelet discretization
\mathbf{A}
) is known exactly,
[
45
, Theorems 10.2–10.3]
show that the proposed matrix-compression strategy preserves the optimal convergence rate of the Galerkin method: for
J>j_{0}
, if
u_{J}
solves the compressed system, then for any
\alpha\in[0,d-r/2]
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\|u_{J}-u\|_{H^{r/2-\alpha}}\lesssim 2^{-J(d+\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{d}},

where
d
is the approximation order of the primal wavelet basis
\Psi
; see also
[
45
, Lemma 3.1]
. This demonstrates that, in the deterministic setting, compression reduces computational cost by sparsifying
\mathbf{A}
while preserving the approximation accuracy of the underlying Galerkin scheme.

Our setting differs from this classical framework in several crucial ways:

1.

Unknown operator.
The operator
\mathcal{A}
is not available and must be learned from data. The estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is constructed to control the error
\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
. Consequently, the numerical solution
\widehat{u}
reflects not only discretization and compression errors but also statistical errors (omitted-variable bias and variance). The resulting convergence rate therefore depends on the sample size
N
used in the learning stage, which governs the accuracy with which
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
approximates
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

Fixed regularity parameters.
Observe that (
6.6
) has a structural form similar to (
6.7
). In our setting, the indices
t,t^{\prime}
are fixed and determine the metric in which
\mathcal{A}
is learned, thereby directly controlling the attainable accuracy of the PDE solution. In contrast, the deterministic bound (
6.7
) depends on the wavelet approximation order
d
, whereas in the data-driven setting the relevant range of Sobolev regularity scales are prescribed by the operator-learning problem through the choice of
t,t^{\prime}
.

3.

Data-dependent resolution level.
The maximal wavelet scale
J_{*}
is chosen as a function of the sample size
N
, balancing truncation, compression and statistical estimation errors. This stands in stark contrast with the classical Galerkin scheme, where
J
is a purely numerical refinement parameter independent of data.

In summary, Theorem
6.2
shows that —even in the data-driven setting where the operator
\mathcal{A}
must be learned— the resulting PDE solver attains a convergence rate that mirrors the nearly optimal statistical rate of operator learning. Moreover, the bound (
6.6
) exhibits a structural form analogous to the classical approximation result (
6.7
), thereby extending the classical wavelet–Galerkin methods with matrix compression to a statistical setting.
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

The proof proceeds in three steps. In
Step 1
, we verify that the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is
V_{J_{*}}
-elliptic. Given this, in
Step 2
we prove the claim for
\alpha=0
by applying Strang’s first lemma
[
32
]
. In
Step 3
, we treat the case
\alpha\in(0,t^{\prime}-r/2]
using the
\alpha=0
estimate established in
Step 2
.

Step 1:
V_{J_{*}}
ellipticity of estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

Given
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{J_{*}}\times\Lambda_{J_{*}}}
, we define the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
as

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{J_{*}}\times\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

It follows from (
6.1
) that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{t-r/2}\,\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}\,\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{t-r/2}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{t-r/2}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{t-r/2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{2J_{*}(t-r/2)}\cdot\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(2t-r)}=\varepsilon.

Moreover,

\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\|\leq\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-t}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J}}^{-t}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{t-r/2}\|\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}.

By Proposition
3.2

(II)
(i), there exists
c_{-}>0
such that

\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-r/2})\geq c_{-}.

Since the spectrum of
\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}
is contained in that of
\mathbf{A}
, it follows that

\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2})\geq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{D}^{-r/2})\geq c_{-}.

Then, by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, we obtain the
V_{J_{*}}
-ellipticity of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
in (
6.8
): for any
u_{J_{*}}\in V_{J_{*}}\subset H^{r/2}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}u_{J_{*}},u_{J_{*}}\rangle=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}},\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}},\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}(\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}-\widehat{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}},\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2})\cdot\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\|^{2}-\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}})\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{-r/2}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(c_{-}-\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda_{J_{*}}}^{r/2}\mathbf{u}_{J_{*}}\|^{2}

(6.9)

\displaystyle\asymp\|u_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{r/2}},

where the last step follows from Lemma
A.1
(iv) and
r/2\in(-\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma)
.

Step 2:
\alpha=0
.
We first prove the case
\alpha=0
. Using (
6.2
), we apply Strang’s first lemma
[
32
, Theorem 4.1.1]
to obtain that

(6.10)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim\inf_{v_{J_{*}}\in V_{J_{*}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v_{J_{*}}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}+\sup_{w_{J}\in V_{J_{*}},w_{J_{*}}\neq 0}\frac{|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})v_{J_{*}},w_{J_{*}}\rangle|}{\|w_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{r/2}}}}}\right\}.

By (
6.1
), we have

|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})v_{J_{*}},w_{J_{*}}\rangle|\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\|v_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{t}}\|w_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}.

Moreover, Lemma
A.1
(ii) yields that, for
r/2<t^{\prime}\leq\gamma
,

\|w_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\lesssim 2^{J_{*}(t^{\prime}-r/2)}\|w_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{r/2}}.

Hence, taking
v_{J_{*}}=Q_{J_{*}}u\in V_{J_{*}}
in (
6.10
) yields that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}+\sup_{w_{J_{*}}\in V_{J_{*}},w_{J_{*}}\neq 0}\frac{|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})Q_{J_{*}}u,w_{J_{*}}\rangle|}{\|w_{J_{*}}\|_{H^{r/2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}+2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\cdot 2^{J_{*}(t^{\prime}-r/2)}\|Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{t}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}},

where the last step follows from
\|Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{t}}\leq\|u\|_{H^{t}}
and
\|u-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}
by Lemma
A.1
(ii), provided that
-\widetilde{d}\leq r/2<t\leq d
,
r/2<\gamma
, and
-\widetilde{\gamma}<t
.

Step 3:
\alpha\in(0,t^{\prime}-r/2]
.

We begin with

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2-\alpha}}=\sup_{g\in H^{\alpha-r/2}}\frac{|\langle\widehat{u}-u,g\rangle|}{\|g\|_{H^{\alpha-r/2}}}.

Since
\mathcal{A}:H^{\alpha+r/2}\to H^{\alpha-r/2}
is an isomorphism, let
v\in H^{\alpha+r/2}
satisfy
\mathcal{A}v=g
, then

(6.11)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2-\alpha}}=\sup_{v\in H^{\alpha+r/2}}\frac{|\langle\widehat{u}-u,\mathcal{A}v\rangle|}{\|\mathcal{A}v\|_{H^{\alpha-r/2}}}\asymp\sup_{v\in H^{\alpha+r/2}}\frac{|\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v\rangle|}{\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}}}.

Using the orthogonality
\langle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{u},Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle=\langle Q_{J_{*}}f,Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle=\langle f,Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle=\langle\mathcal{A}u,Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle
, we can decompose

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v-Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle+\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle

(6.12)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v-Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle+\langle(\mathcal{A}-\widehat{\mathcal{A}})\widehat{u},Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle.

The first term on the right-hand side of (
6.2
) is bounded by

(6.13)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v-Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle|\lesssim\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}\|v-Q_{J_{*}}v\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t+\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}},

where in the second inequality we used the bound
\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}
obtained in
Step 2
, and
\|v-Q_{J_{*}}v\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}\alpha}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}}
by Lemma
A.1
(ii), provided that
-\widetilde{d}\leq r/2\leq\alpha+r/2\leq d
,
r/2<\gamma
, and
-\widetilde{\gamma}<\alpha+r/2
. These conditions are guaranteed by
r/2\in(-\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma)
,
\alpha\in(0,t^{\prime}-r/2]
, and
t^{\prime}\leq d
.

For the second term in (
6.2
),

\displaystyle|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})\widehat{u},Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})(\widehat{u}-Q_{J_{*}}u),Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle|+|\langle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})Q_{J_{*}}u,Q_{J_{*}}v\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{r/2}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\|\widehat{u}-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}\|Q_{J_{*}}v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}+\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\|Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{t}}\|Q_{J_{*}}v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t^{\prime}-r/2)}\cdot 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}\cdot 2^{J_{*}(t^{\prime}-\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}}

\displaystyle\quad+2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)}\cdot 2^{J_{*}(t^{\prime}-\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}}

(6.14)

\displaystyle\asymp 2^{-J_{*}(t+\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}},

where we used the following inequalities:

\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{r/2}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t^{\prime}-r/2)},\qquad\|\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A}\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t+t^{\prime}-r)},

\|Q_{J_{*}}v\|_{H^{t^{\prime}}}\lesssim 2^{J_{*}(t^{\prime}-\alpha-r/2)}\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}},\quad\text{ if }\alpha+r/2\leq t^{\prime}\leq\gamma,

and

\|\widehat{u}-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}\leq\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2}}+\|u-Q_{J_{*}}u\|_{H^{r/2}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}.

Combining (
6.11
), (
6.2
), (
6.13
), and (
6.2
) yields that, for any
\alpha\in(0,t^{\prime}-r/2]
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{u}-u\|_{H^{r/2-\alpha}}\asymp\sup_{v\in H^{\alpha+r/2}}\frac{|\langle\mathcal{A}(\widehat{u}-u),v\rangle|}{\|v\|_{H^{\alpha+r/2}}}\lesssim 2^{-J_{*}(t+\alpha-r/2)}\|u\|_{H^{t}}\asymp_{\mathrm{polylog}}N^{-\frac{t+\alpha-r/2}{(2+\rho)(t+t^{\prime}-r)}}\|u\|_{H^{t}},

where the last step follows from the choice of
J_{*}
in (
6.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

7
Conclusions, Discussion, and Future Directions

This paper has established convergence rates for learning elliptic pseudo-differential operators from noisy (and noiseless) data. Within a wavelet–Galerkin framework, we formulated the learning task as a structured infinite-dimensional regression problem with multiscale sparsity. Building on this structure, we proposed a sparse, data- and computation-efficient estimator that combines a learning-oriented matrix compression scheme with a nested-support regression strategy to balance approximation and estimation errors. In addition to obtaining convergence rates for the estimator, we showed that the learned operator induces an efficient and stable Galerkin solver whose numerical error inherits its statistical accuracy. Our results therefore contribute to bringing together operator learning, data-driven solvers, and wavelet methods in scientific computing.

We conclude with several open questions and future directions that arise from this work.

Ellipticity, data/noise distribution, computation, and error metric

In this paper we assumed that
\mathcal{A}
is strongly elliptic, whereas the wavelet-coordinate estimates in Proposition
3.2
(I) hold more generally for PDOs beyond the elliptic class. It would be interesting to relax the ellipticity assumption and understand what weaker conditions suffice for the learning task. To simplify the exposition, we also focused on the setting where both the inputs and the noise are Gaussian random functions. Since our error analysis relies primarily on the covariance properties in Proposition
3.2
(II) (ii)-(iii), it is natural to expect that both the estimation procedure and the resulting convergence guarantees extend beyond Gaussianity —for instance, to settings where the input and noise distributions satisfy appropriate finite-moment (or sub-Gaussian/sub-exponential) assumptions. On the computational side, we noted at the end of Remark
5.5
that it would be interesting to improve the runtime to an exact
\mathcal{O}(N2^{Jn})
while preserving the same statistical rate. Finally, regarding the error metric, throughout the paper we measured the estimation accuracy in the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{t}\to H^{-t^{\prime}}}
. One may also consider a prediction/generalization criterion under a testing distribution. For example, for a Gaussian test input
v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{C}_{v})
, one can study

\mathbb{E}_{v}\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})v\big\|_{H^{-t^{\prime}}}.

Suppose that the wavelet representation
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{v}
of
\mathcal{C}_{v}
satisfies an analogue of Proposition
3.2
(II) (ii), namely, for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

c_{-}\leq\sigma_{\min}\big(\mathbf{D}^{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{v}\mathbf{D}^{t}\big)\leq\sigma_{\max}\big(\mathbf{D}^{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{v}\mathbf{D}^{t}\big)\leq c_{+}.

Then a standard covariance computation yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{v}\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\mathcal{A})v\big\|_{H^{-t^{\prime}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\big(\mathbf{D}^{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{v}\mathbf{D}^{t}\big)\big(\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\big)^{\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\mathbf{D}^{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{v}\mathbf{D}^{t}\big\|\,\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{D}^{-t^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{D}^{-t}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
denotes the Hilbert–Schmidt norm on operators
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})\to\ell^{2}(\mathcal{J})
. The multiscale techniques developed in this paper can be adapted to obtain convergence rates under such Hilbert–Schmidt-type criteria as well; we leave a detailed treatment to future work.

Adaptive operator estimation and learning

An important avenue for future research is
adaptive
operator estimation and learning.
A guiding principle behind our learning methodology is the availability of wavelet-coordinate decay estimates for
\mathcal{A}
(Proposition
3.2
(I)), which inform learning-oriented compression and regression procedures that exploit multiscale sparsity and aim to estimate only the significant entries so as to balance approximation (bias) and estimation (variance) errors.
Throughout this paper, we assume the order of
\mathcal{A}
is known.
It is therefore natural to ask whether one can develop methods that adapt to an unknown order, and hence to an unknown sparsity pattern.
Concretely, instead of selecting a support via explicit thresholding parameters
\tau_{jj^{\prime}}
and slope conditions as in Definition
4.2
, can one learn the effective support (i.e., the significant coordinates) from the data in an adaptive manner?

Related questions are classical in scientific computing and numerical analysis, where adaptive methods —including adaptive wavelet schemes
[
33
,
41
,
37
,
131
]
and adaptive finite element methods
[
110
,
8
,
12
,
130
,
13
]
— play a central role in achieving optimal finite-term approximation and sparse representations of PDE solutions.
In the context of operator learning, it is natural to explore analogous notions of adaptivity to the structure of the unknown operator, and to investigate suitable concepts of nonlinear approximation for operator classes.

From a statistical perspective, sparsity-inducing regularization and, in particular, the Lasso and its variants occupy a central place in modern high-dimensional statistics
[
137
,
152
,
21
,
141
,
151
]
.
It would be interesting to establish sparse oracle inequalities —covering variable selection, support recovery, and estimation— for operator learning problems such as those studied here.

Learning other structured operator classes

This paper establishes convergence rates for learning elliptic PDOs in wavelet coordinates by leveraging
a priori
structure that yields multiscale compressibility and enables sparsity-based estimation in an infinite-dimensional setting. The methodology and analysis developed here may extend to other operator classes with analogous structured representations. For instance, Fourier integral operators (FIOs) play a central role in wave propagation and hyperbolic PDEs
[
73
,
138
,
63
,
75
,
81
,
148
,
142
]
, and curvelets provide near-optimal sparse representations for broad families of FIOs
[
27
,
25
,
23
,
24
,
22
]
, suggesting that an analogous multiscale, sparsity-based approach could be viable in suitable curvelet coordinates. Related structured operators also arise in parabolic problems
[
140
,
123
,
28
]
. More broadly, and more challengingly, one may aim to learn structured
nonlinear
operators; a prototypical example is the parameter-to-solution map for linear, second-order, divergence-form elliptic PDEs
[
36
,
38
,
118
]
.

Bayesian formulations for operator learning

We have mentioned that
[
54
]
establishes posterior contraction rates for learning linear operators that are diagonalizable in a known basis within a Bayesian framework. More broadly, Bayesian formulations can quantify uncertainty in the learned operator and propagate it to downstream numerical predictions, yielding posterior credible sets for PDE solutions and related functionals. A key question is whether one can leverage the same multiscale compressibility that underpins our frequentist rates to design computationally tractable priors for structured operator classes (e.g., sparsity- or shrinkage-based priors in wavelet coordinates) and to establish corresponding posterior contraction guarantees. We believe that developing a general Bayesian framework for operator learning —encompassing broader structured operator classes beyond the diagonalizable setting and providing contraction rates (and, ideally, frequentist coverage properties of credible sets)— is a promising direction for future work.

Appendix A
Auxiliary Materials Section
3

A.1
Background: Biorthogonal Wavelets and Multiresolution Analysis

Let
\{V_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
be a sequence of nested, linear finite-dimensional subspaces
V_{j}\subset V_{j+1}\subset\cdots\subset L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
. We say that the family
\{V_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
has
regularity

\gamma>0
and
(approximation) order

d\in\mathbb{N}
if

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{s\in\mathbb{R}:V_{j}\subset H^{s}(\mathcal{M}),\ \forall\ j>j_{0}}}\right\},

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{s\in\mathbb{R}:\inf_{v_{j}\in V_{j}}\|v-v_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})}\lesssim 2^{-js}\|v\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})},\ \forall\ v\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M}),\ \forall\ j>j_{0}}}\right\}.

We shall suppose that the subspaces
\{V_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
are
H^{r/2}(\mathcal{M})
-conforming, i.e., we have
\gamma>\max\{0,r/2\}
for some fixed order
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

We furthermore assume that
\mathrm{dim}(V_{j})=O(2^{nj})
and that, for each
j>j_{0}
, the space
V_{j}
is spanned by a single-scale basis
\Phi_{j}
, i.e.,

V_{j}=\mathrm{span}\,\Phi_{j},\quad\mathrm{where}\ \,\Phi_{j}:=\{\phi_{j,k}:k\in\Delta_{j}\},\quad\forall\ j>j_{0}.

Here, the index set
\Delta_{j}
describes the spatial localization of elements in
\Phi_{j}
. Analogously
to the spaces
\{V_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
, we assume without loss of generality that the finite index sets
\{\Delta_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
are nested,
\Delta_{j}\subset\Delta_{j+1}\subset\cdots
. For each
j>j_{0}
, we associate with
\Phi_{j}
the
dual single-scale basis
defined by

\widetilde{\Phi}_{j}:=\{\widetilde{\phi}_{j,k}:k\in\Delta_{j}\},\quad\mathrm{with}\ \,\langle\phi_{j,k},\widetilde{\phi}_{j,k^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{k,k^{\prime}},\ \forall\ k,k^{\prime}\in\Delta_{j}.

The vector spaces
\widetilde{V}_{j}:=\mathrm{span}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{j},\ j>j_{0}
, are also nested,
\widetilde{V}_{j}\subset\widetilde{V}_{j+1}\subset\cdots\subset L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and the family
\{\widetilde{V}_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
provides regularity
\widetilde{\gamma}>0
and approximation order
\widetilde{d}
. For example, let the primal bases
\Phi_{j}
be generated by tensor products of univariate
\mathrm{B}
-splines of order
d
, and let the dual bases
\widetilde{\Phi}_{j}
be of order
\widetilde{d}\geq d
, with
d+\widetilde{d}
even. Then
V_{j}=\mathrm{span}\,\Phi_{j}
and
\widetilde{V}_{j}=\mathrm{span}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{j}
have approximation orders
d
and
\widetilde{d}
, respectively. Moreover, the corresponding regularity indices satisfy
\gamma=d-1/2
, while
\widetilde{\gamma}>0
grows proportionally with
\widetilde{d}
. We refer to
[
46
,
50
,
114
,
115
,
119
]
for detailed constructions.

In view of the biorthogonality of
\Phi_{j},\widetilde{\Phi}_{j}
, we define the canonical projectors

Q_{j}v:=\sum_{k\in\Delta_{j}}\langle v,\widetilde{\phi}_{j,k}\rangle\phi_{j,k},\qquad Q^{*}_{j}v:=\sum_{k\in\Delta_{j}}\langle v,\phi_{j,k}\rangle\widetilde{\phi}_{j,k},\quad\forall\ v\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M}),

associated with the multiresolution sequences
\{V_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}},\{\widetilde{V}_{j}\}_{j>j_{0}}
. Moreover, the
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
-boundedness of
Q_{j}
implies the Jackson and Bernstein inequalities; see Lemma
A.1
(ii) below.

To define
multiresolution analyses
(MRA), we start by introducing index sets
\nabla_{j}:=\Delta_{j+1}\backslash\Delta_{j},\,j>j_{0}
. Given single-scale bases
\Phi_{j}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{j}
, one can construct
biorthogonal complement bases

\Psi_{j}=\{\psi_{j,k}:k\in\nabla_{j}\}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\widetilde{\Psi}_{j}=\{\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}:k\in\nabla_{j}\},\qquad j>j_{0},

satisfying the
biorthogonality relation

\langle\psi_{j,k},\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}\rangle=\delta_{(j,k),(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j=j^{\prime}\text{ and }k=k^{\prime},\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

such that

\mathrm{diam}\bigl(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k})\bigr)\asymp 2^{-j},\quad j>j_{0}.

We refer to
[
114
,
115
,
119
]
for particular constructions. In addition, we use the convention
\Psi_{j_{0}}:=\Phi_{j_{0}+1},\widetilde{\Psi}_{j_{0}}:=\widetilde{\Phi}_{j_{0}+1}
, and
\nabla_{j_{0}}:=\Delta_{j_{0}+1}
.

For
j>j_{0}
, define
W_{j}:=\mathrm{span}\,\Psi_{j}
and
\widetilde{W}_{j}:=\mathrm{span}\,\widetilde{\Psi}_{j}
. The biorthogonality implies that, for all
j>j_{0}
,

V_{j+1}=W_{j}\oplus V_{j},\quad\widetilde{V}_{j+1}=\widetilde{W}_{j}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{j},\quad\widetilde{V}_{j}\perp W_{j},\quad V_{j}\perp\widetilde{W}_{j}.

Hence,
V_{J}
and
\widetilde{V}_{J}
can be written as a direct sum of the complement spaces
W_{j}
, respectively,
\widetilde{W}_{j}
,
j_{0}\leq j<J
, using the convention
W_{j_{0}}:=V_{j_{0}+1}
and
\widetilde{W}_{j_{0}}:=\widetilde{V}_{j_{0}+1}
. With the convention
Q_{j_{0}}=Q^{*}_{j_{0}}:=0
, one has for
v_{J}\in V_{J}
and for
\widetilde{v}_{J}\in\widetilde{V}_{J}
that

v_{J}=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}(Q_{j+1}-Q_{j})v_{J},\qquad\widetilde{v}_{J}=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}(Q^{*}_{j+1}-Q^{*}_{j})\widetilde{v}_{J},

where

(Q_{j+1}-Q_{j})v=\sum_{k\in\nabla_{j}}\langle v,\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}\rangle\psi_{j,k},\qquad(Q^{*}_{j+1}-Q^{*}_{j})v=\sum_{k\in\nabla_{j}}\langle v,\psi_{j,k}\rangle\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}.

From this observation, a biorthogonal dual pair of wavelet bases is now obtained from the union of the coarse single-scale basis and the complement bases,

\Psi=\bigcup_{j\geq j_{0}}\Psi_{j},\qquad\widetilde{\Psi}=\bigcup_{j\geq j_{0}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{j}.

We refer to
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
as the
primal
and
dual
MRAs, respectively. Here and throughout, all basis functions in
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
are assumed to be normalized in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
.

The next lemma collects several key properties of the biorthogonal wavelet
system
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
that will be used throughout our operator
learning analysis; see

[
51
,
46
,
33
,
68
]
.

Lemma A.1

(Properties of biorthogonal wavelets)
.

Let
(\Psi,\widetilde{\Psi})
be a biorthogonal wavelet system on
\mathcal{M}

with parameters
(\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma},d,\widetilde{d})
. Then the following hold.

(i)

Cardinality and locality.
For each
j\geq j_{0}
, the number of wavelets at level
j
satisfies
|\nabla_{j}|\asymp 2^{jn}
. Moreover, the supports are localized in the sense that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{supp}\,\psi_{j,k})\asymp 2^{-j},
and there exists a constant
M
such that, for every
k\in\nabla_{j}
, at most
M
indices
k^{\prime}\in\nabla_{j}
satisfy
\mathrm{meas}\big(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k})\cap\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k^{\prime}})\big)\neq 0.

(ii)

Approximation and stability.
Let
Q_{j}
denote the canonical projector onto the approximation space
V_{j}
. We take
Q_{j_{0}}=0
and, for
j>j_{0}
,

\displaystyle Q_{j}v=\sum_{j^{\prime}=j_{0}}^{j-1}\sum_{k\in\nabla_{j^{\prime}}}\langle v,\widetilde{\psi}_{j^{\prime},k}\rangle\psi_{j^{\prime},k},\quad\forall\ v\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M}).

The Jackson and Bernstein inequalities hold:

\|v-Q_{j}v\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})}\lesssim 2^{-j(t-s)}\|v\|_{H^{t}(\mathcal{M})},\quad\forall\ v\in H^{t}(\mathcal{M}),

for all
-\widetilde{d}\leq s\leq t\leq d,s<\gamma,-\widetilde{\gamma}<t
, and

\|Q_{j}v\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})}\lesssim 2^{j(s-t)}\|Q_{j}v\|_{H^{t}(\mathcal{M})},\quad\forall\ v\in H^{t}(\mathcal{M}),

for all
t\leq s\leq\gamma
.

(iii)

Vanishing moments.
For any sufficiently smooth function
v
,

\displaystyle|\langle v,\psi_{j,k}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{-j(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}|v|_{W^{\widetilde{d},\infty}(\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k}))},\quad|v|_{W^{\widetilde{d},\infty}(\Omega)}:=\sup_{|\alpha|=\widetilde{d},x\in\Omega}|\partial^{\alpha}v(x)|.

(iv)

Norm equivalence.
For any
v\in H^{t}(\mathcal{M})
, the following equivalences hold:

\displaystyle\|v\|_{H^{t}(\mathcal{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle\asymp\sum_{j\geq j_{0}}\sum_{k\in\nabla_{j}}2^{2jt}|\langle v,\widetilde{\psi}_{j,k}\rangle|^{2},\quad t\in(-\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma),

\displaystyle\|v\|_{H^{t}(\mathcal{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle\asymp\sum_{j\geq j_{0}}\sum_{k\in\nabla_{j}}2^{2jt}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v,\psi_{j,k}}}\right\rangle}}\right|^{2},\quad t\in(-\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}).

A.2
Properties of
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{U},\mathbf{W}

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

(I)

Approximate sparsity:
The estimate in (i) is well known in the literature
[
51
,
45
,
122
,
68
]
: it shows that the matrix entries decay rapidly when the supports of the testing wavelets are well separated. For the reader’s convenience, we include a brief and formal derivation.

Let
k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{i(x-y)\cdot\xi}a(x,\xi)d\xi
be the (distributional) Schwartz kernel of
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}
. When
\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})\gtrsim 2^{-\min\{j,j^{\prime}\}}
, the product
\psi_{j,k}(x)\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(y)
is supported away from the diagonal
x=y
, hence
k_{\mathcal{A}}
is smooth on that set and Fubini’s theorem applies. Therefore,

\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}},\psi_{j,k}\rangle=\iint k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)\psi_{j,k}(x)\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(y)dxdy=\int\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(y)dy}}\right)\psi_{j,k}(x)dx.

By Lemma
A.1
(iii) (vanishing moments up to order
\widetilde{d}
),

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}},\psi_{j,k}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{-j(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\sup_{|\alpha|=\widetilde{d},x\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int\partial_{x}^{\alpha}k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(y)dy}}\right|.

Applying the same argument in the
y
–variable yields

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}},\psi_{j,k}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{-(j+j^{\prime})(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\sup_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k}),y\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}),|\alpha|=|\beta|=\widetilde{d}}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)|.

For
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}
, the kernel satisfies the standard off–diagonal bounds (see
[
68
,
51
,
45
,
122
]
)

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}k_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\mathrm{dist}(x,y)^{-(n+r+|\alpha|+|\beta|)},\quad x\neq y,

hence

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}},\psi_{j,k}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-(j+j^{\prime})(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\sup_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j,k}),y\in\mathrm{supp}(\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}),|\alpha|=|\beta|=\widetilde{d}}\mathrm{dist}(x,y)^{-(n+r+|\alpha|+|\beta|)}

\displaystyle=2^{-(j+j^{\prime})(\widetilde{d}+n/2)}\mathrm{dist}(S_{j,k},S_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}})^{-(n+r+2\widetilde{d})}.

We refer the reader to
[
68
, Proposition 2]
and
[
51
,
122
]
for further background.

The estimate in (ii) shows that matrix entries corresponding to wavelets with overlapping supports decay as the difference in scales increases; see
[
51
, Section 9.4]
,
[
33
, (2.28)]
, and
[
45
, Remark, p.2261]
. We provide a brief derivation on this estimate following
[
51
, Section 9.4]
.

Without loss of generality, assume
j\geq j^{\prime}
. Under Assumption
2.1
(i), the operator
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{OPS}^{r}(\mathcal{M})
acts continuously and bijectively from
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
to
H^{s-r}(\mathcal{M})
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
; see
[
68
, Proposition 11]
.
Using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality together with the bound
\|\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\|_{H^{-r/2+\sigma}}\lesssim\|\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\|_{H^{r/2+\sigma}}
, we obtain

(A.1)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}},\psi_{j,k}\rangle|\leq\|\mathcal{A}\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\|_{H^{-r/2+\sigma}}\|\psi_{j,k}\|_{H^{r/2-\sigma}}\lesssim\|\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\|_{H^{r/2+\sigma}}\|\psi_{j,k}\|_{H^{r/2-\sigma}}.

If
r/2+\sigma<\gamma
and
r/2-\sigma>-\widetilde{\gamma}
, the norm equivalence in Lemma
A.1
(iv) can be applied to each factor on the right-hand side of (
A.1
). Using biorthogonality then yields
